FOREWORD 


Tue diary of Lady Margaret Hoby, printed in the following 
pages, has been transcribed and edited from the original which 
is preserved amongst the manuscripts at the British Museum. 
It is numbered Egerton 2614, but nothing is known of its 
history beyond the fact that it was purchased, by the trustees 
of the Museum, from the Rev. C. St. B. Sydenham on roth 
November, 1883. 

The manuscript consists of a hundred and eighteen folios, 
each measuring 5,%, inches in breadth by 7,4, inches in length. 
The first two folios and the last one are torn, so that it is impossible 
to say whether the whole of the diary has been preserved. More- 
over, it has now been put into a modern cover, and, in addition, 
the inner edge of each folio has been bound with a narrow Strip, 
the stitching of the binding being done then through these strips. 

Each folio is written on both sides, but the diary covers only 
a few years of its author’s life—from August, 1599, to July, 1605. 
The long full entries of the early part would seem to indicate 
Lady Hoby’s interest in a new pastime of which she afterwards 
came to weary. The entries from 1oth August, 1599, to that 
date in 1600 occupy fifty folios; thence, to August, 1601, 
thirty-five ; while August, 1601, to July, 1605, is all comprised 
in folios 85-118. 

The water-mark in the paper upon which Lady Hoby wrote 
her diary is the same throughout the book. It appears to be a kind 
of Greek vase with a bunch of grapes at one end. The mark 
usually occurs at the edge of the page, in about the middle, and 
cut in half or a quarter of its length by the binding. It does not 
show on every page, but, taking @ and + to represent the 
two ends of the design, is found as follows for the first 
thirty folios: none, a, none, a, a, 4, b, none, 4, none, none, 
a, none, none, a, a, none, none, 4, 4, none, none, J, a, none, 
b, none, a, none, none. 

In some parts of the diary the writing is much faded, and in 
others the ink has stained through from one side of the page 


ix 


x FOREWORD 


to the other. In places it is still a good black; elsewhere, the 
green in it is very distinét. The hand-writing throughout 1s 
that of the diarist herself, Lady Margaret Hoby of Hackness, 
Yorkshire, the wife of Sir Thomas Posthumous Hoby. She 
wrote in a current Italic hand, which is sometimes very bold, 
and shows few Secretary forms. It is the calligraphy of one well 
accustomed to the use of the pen. “The page reproduced shows 
its chara¢teristics. It varies in size throughout the diary, becoming 
on occasions very large and thick. It can be small and exquisitely 
neat, especially when Lady Hoby squeezes in a last line or two 
at the foot of a page. ‘The letters are not all made consistently 
throughout, and she made use of the strokes of abbreviation in 
common use at this period. Occasionally, when the edge of a page 
cramped her for space, the ending of a word disappeared 


completely. 
Sometimes the hooded form of the letter @ occurs, but the 
diary contains few such Secretary forms. At times it was 


found impossible to decide when the initial L or V was intended 
by the writer for a capital letter. Size has been made the deter- 
mining factor, especially as regards the tail of the ZL, for this letter 
is more or less the same throughout the diary. “The usual 
dificulty over » and uw cropped up: it was hard to know, 
for instance, if Lady Hoby intended to write soun or sonn. 
Sometimes it was fairly obviously the latter. She usually wrote 
gonn or gone. Sometimes a capital letter occurs in the 
middle of a word. 

Smudges, blots, crossings-out, alterations, the stains of age, 
all decorate the diary, rendering it in parts almost indecipherable. 
Throughout, however, Lady Hoby keeps a wide and perfectly 
Straight margin, for, though no line is drawn, the beginning of 
each line of writing is exa¢tly beneath another. “This margin is 
practically identical in width through the book. Her lines of 
writing, too, run precisely straight across the page though no 
ruler, apparently, was used. Occasionally there is a comment 
in the margin, but usually this is occupied only by the dates of her 
entries. “I’hese dates have in many places been corrected, though 
sometimes an inaccuracy has gone unnoticed. Frequently a word 
or words are written in above the line, not always over an omission 
mark. When occasion arises for the division of a word, it is often 
done very quaintly, e.g. brea on one line, 4fast on another. 


FOREWORD xi 


The first and last two pages of the diary are badly torn as 
will be seen by the text. Otherwise the little volume is almost 
perfect. 

Lady Hoby’s spelling was far from consistent and the marks 
of punctuation she used were few. The period is found, 
occasionally in conjunétion with the virgula, a comma here and 
there, and a colon before and after most of the numerals. Often 
the 7’s and z’s go uncrossed and undotted. This insufficiency 
of punctuation has perforce been remedied, with as little violence 
to the text as possible, in order to make the perusal of the diary 
more pleasant. An attempt has been made to keep to the colon, 
comma, and period, for with these Lady Hoby was familiar. 
‘The entries of the diary rarely conclude with any sort of pun¢tua- 
tion mark, except towards the end of the book, when the virgula 
has been generally adopted. Lady Hoby’s abbreviations have, 
where possible, been extended ; words written in above the line 
have been put into their right place in the text; in all other 
respects the original diary has been faithfully transcribed. It is, 
in itself, a remarkably interesting indication of the customary attain- 
ments of the women of the period ; hence as much of the original 
as possible has been kept in this modern reproduétion, for it is 
throughout in the handwriting of one person. 

The distri in Yorkshire with which Lady Hoby was 
familiar, and the London in which for a time she stayed, have 
been illustrated by maps. Her journeys to London and York 
are shown by means of road-rolls, a form of diagram used in 
England for the first time in the road-books of her period. 

A bibliography of the sources used for the notes upon the 
text is given at the end of the book. 

I am deeply indebted to Dr. Eileen Power for her unfailing 
help, advice, and encouragement, and to Miss Warren for the 
very careful drawings of the road-rolls. Holbein’s portrait of 
Lady Russell is at Windsor Castle. By gracious permission 
of His Majesty the King it is reproduced in this book. 


INTRODUCTION 


Six miles inland, and a little to the north-west of the ancient 
port of Scarborough, lies the tiny but delightful village of Hackness : 
at the time of the last census it contained only some one hundred 
and sixty inhabitants. Resting immediately under steep 
surrounding hills, in a thickly and beautifully wooded valley, 
the little township may be approached through several clefts 
in those heights, by roads which all descend into it, some steeply, 
some gently, from the pleasant dales and bleak but lovely moors 
which lie around. If we enter the village by road, over wide- 
stretched Sylpho moor, from lonely Harwood Dale, 4 miles 
away to the north but still part of Hackness parish, our way is 
made hideous by large white boards, proclaiming in big black 
letters: PRIVATE PROPERTY. On the other side, 
however, the picturesque Forge Valley makes the approach 
from the south shady and delightful. ‘The river Derwent, 
from which name the present owner of Hackness takes his title, 
widens out in the middle of the village—it is an excellent and 
noted fishing stream—and, viewed from the hills above, reflects 
deeply and mysteriously the green of the fine encircling trees and 
slopes, and the beauty of the slender church spire. Hackness 
is a place of soft but ceaseless sound, of hidden hurrying streams, 
of water over the ancient mill-weir, and the incessant whisper 
of leaves. Nor can it have changed much since Lady Hoby 
lived there, three hundred years ago. 

The church is the one she knew and loved, where she 
worshipped, and in the chancel of which she is buried. But 
her home, that old timbered manor-house where the greater 
part of her married life was spent, and her diary written, 
was pulled down in 1798, because it obscured the view from the 
new hall, completed in that year. ‘The site of the old house is 
to the south-west of the beautiful grounds of the present 
mansion, and close to the church.1 

B 


2 DIARY OFVLADY AO Din 


The beginnings of this church are of a very early date, perhaps 
of the later pre-Conquest period, the building consisting at first, 
probably, of an aisleless nave and chancel. ‘There are within it 
now some Anglo-Saxon remains, such as the fragments of a huge 
cross, sculptured with regular scrolls and symmetrical plaits, 
with figures and beasts, and inscriptions in Latin and in Anglian 
runes. [he imposgt of the chancel arch also shows Saxon or 
pre-Norman decoration.? The nave of this early church is to-day 
represented by the present nave within the arcades; it has walls 
2. feet in thickness. Added to little by little, this church before 
Lady Hoby came to the village had become much as it is now, 
with the pointed arches of the north, and rounded arches of the 
south, arcades, the twelfth century tower with its later embattled 
parapet, and the fifteenth century chancel, clerestory, and o¢tagonal 
spire. “Lhe vestry, dating from the early seventeenth century, 
was perhaps built by the generosity of Lady Margaret and her 
husband, Sir Thomas Posthumous Hoby, who certainly repaired 
the chancel in 1597.3 Though the whole building as it now 
Stands has been much restored, yet several of its monuments 
still proclaim the woman in whose life it played so great a part. 
It may be that the old arm-rests and miserere seats of the Stalls 
in the chancel were familiar to her, and that the elaborate cover 
of the font gave her pleasure when she attended the baptisms 
in the church, simple services often accompanied by one of her 
much-loved sermons. 

The village of Hackness has an interesting, though uneventful, 
history. It seems to have been granted by King Oswy to Hilda, 
abbess and foundress of Whitby Abbey, who is said by Bede to 
have founded a monastery there. During the Danish period, 
the abbey lost apparently everything except Hackness. After- 
wards, before 1078, a second monastery was founded at Whitby 
on the site of the earlier one by the Benediétine monk and quasi- 
soldier Reinfrid, who acquired from William de Percy for his 
foundation the ruined monastery and more land, retaining also 
the church and lands in Hackness. “The boundaries of this 
property, which, with its accompanying privileges, formed a 
district known as the liberty of Whitby Strand, were confirmed 
to the abbot and monks of Whitby by Henry I and later kings. 
Generally some of the monks of Whitby lived at Hackness, 


where, it was written, “‘ our monks serve God, die, and are buried.”’ 


Di mw) OM VAD Y VHOBY 3 


The supposed site of their dwelling was, like that of the old manor- 
house, to the south-west of the grounds of the present hall, but 
nothing remains to show whether the monks built another church 
in the village during their sojourn there, though two churches 
at Hackness are certainly mentioned early in the twelfth century. 
When Whitby Abbey surrendered to the Crown in 1539, four 
of its monks were living at the settlement in the beautiful valley. 
The wide lands of the abbey then included the town and port of 
Whitby and its parish church, the dependent chapels at Filing, 
Hawsker, Sneaton, Ugglebarnby, Dunsley and Aislaby, five mills, 
including one at Ruswarp which still exists, and the town, mills, 
and church of Hackness.# 

The whole of the lands which comprised the liberty of Whitby 
Strand, with the exception of Hackness, were, in 1550, granted 
to the Earl of Warwick, who conveyed them, soon afterwards, 
to his supporter, Sir John York, and his wife Anne. ‘They, 
in turn, sold to Sir Richard Cholmley, who was succeeded in 
his possession by his son Francis, and, in 1593, by Francis’s half- 
brother Henry. Henry was knighted in 1603. He lived at 
Roxby. In Lady Hoby’s diary we meet both him and his son 
Richard, who lived at Whitby, and who was also knighted in 
1603. It was in Sir Henry’s time that lawsuits began as to 
whether the courts, as well as the lands, of the liberty had been 
included in the original grant to the Earl of Warwick. ‘The 
Cholmleys had held these courts, and pocketed the proceeds of 
justice, from the time that they had bought the lands. Now 
the Crown claimed them, and finally, at his own request, Sir 
‘Thomas Posthumous Hoby of Hackness, the neighbour whom the 
Cholmleys so mnch disliked, was granted the bailiwick of the 
liberty for life in 1609. “The Cholmleys, however, put in a 
counter-claim, and the matter was finally decided in their favour, 
though Sir Thomas was confirmed in his right to hold the Courts 
Leet and Baron of his independent manor.®> For Hackness, as we 
have seen, had not passed with the liberty of Whitby Strand to 
the Earl of Warwick. Instead, the possessions there of the 
abbey, i.e. the manor, the mills, and the rectory and advowson 
of the church, were granted by the Crown, in 1563, to Lord 
Robert Dudley, the Queen’s favourite. In the following year 
Dudley conveyed the property to Sir John Constable of Burton 
Constable,® and in 1589 it was purchased by three gentlemen, 


4 DIARY OF: LADY HOBBY 


Arthur Dakins, Esq., the Earl of Essex, and the Earl of 
Huntingdon. They bought it in trust for Walter Devereux, 
brother of the Earl of Essex, and his wife Margaret, who was 
the only daughter of Arthur Dakins, a Yorkshire landowner.’ 
Margaret is the heroine of our story, and the author of the diary. 

The name Dakins does not appear in Yorkshire until the 
sixteenth century, and then first in the neighbourhood of Hull.8 
In 1536 and after, one George Dakyn or Dawkens was atting 
as servant to Richard Cromwell, nephew of Henry VIII’s Vicar- 
General, and transa¢ting his business in Yorkshire,® and in 1539 
this same man was granted a lease for twenty-one years of the 
manor of Settrington, Yorkshire, with divers parcels of demesne 
lands once belonging to Sir Francis Bygod.1° ‘The records of 
the Court of Augmentations show him, in 1541, speculating 
in lands which had formerly been the possession of monastic 
houses.11. ‘This individual was a member of the Derbyshire 
family of Dakins to which Margaret’s father also belonged. 
Several branches of this family settled in the North and East 
Ridings of Yorkshire at the time of the Reformation, becoming 
wealthy by speculating in, or being rewarded for services by 
grants of, the confiscated lands of religious houses, a kind of origin 
to which many of the landed gentry of to-day can look back. 

Margaret’s parents, Arthur Dakins and his wife, were regarded 
as “strangers”? in the neighbourhood of Linton in the East 
Riding where they lived.12 This district was still, in the middle 
of the sixteenth century, and for long after, a papist stronghold. 
‘The Visitation of Yorkshire in 1584—5, which gives the pedigree 
of the Dakins of Linton, states that Margaret’s grandfather’s 
name was Robert, but furnishes no particulars concerning him. 
‘The family was granted arms only twenty-five years before this 
date, which would seem to indicate that it had only recently 
become important. Arthur Dakins’s elder brother Humphrey 
is described as of Chelmorton, Derbyshire, but Humphrey’s 
son and grandson are later to be found at Linton, though his 
sister married a Derbyshire man. At this same time also, George 
Dakins, the head of another branch of the family, lived at Foul- 
bridge on the Derwent near Yeddingham, while his son Arthur 
owned the manor of East Cowton.!4 

Arthur Dakins of Linton became a man of PI en ine 
the distri€t, acquiring much land,!5 and rising to be a member of 
the Quorum and a Justice of the Peace, He married ‘Thomasine 


DELANY), OR? LADY HO BY 5 
Guy, or Gye, daughter of Thomas Gye, “ captain of Silye, in 


co. Cumberland,’”? whose mother was one of the Carews of 
Anthony in Cornwall.1® “Their only daughter and heir, Margaret, 
was baptised on roth February 1571 17 in Wintringham Church, 
in which parish the township of Linton, where they then lived, 
was situated. It may be presumed, therefore, that Margaret 
was born in her father’s house at Linton, the place to which her 
diary so often records her visits to her mother. Arthur Dakins had 
acquired land in that place from his cousin George in 1565.18 

When she was old enough, Margaret would seem to have 
been taken into the sternly Puritan household of Catherine, 
Countess of Huntingdon, the wife of Henry Hastings, the third 
Earl.!® A period spent in such service was the usual method at 
this period of educating girls of Margaret’s station in life, and of 
preparing them to adorn the sole vocation open to them—amarriage. 
‘The one, and indeed the only, aim of most Tudor parents, as 
regards their daughters, was to dispose of them, as speedily as 
might be, by arranging an early and, if possible, a wealthy match. 
Thus the household into which a girl was sent was carefully 
chosen with this end in view. If the young lady were herself 
wealthy, almost all doors were eagerly held ajar for her. Now 
Margaret was an heiress, and her sojourn in the Huntingdon 
household was suitably crowned by a matrimonial alliance with 
Walter Devereux, the second son of Walter, Earl of Essex, who 
was cousin to the Earl of Huntingdon. Walter and his two sisters, 
Penelope and Dorothy Devereux, were also being trained under 
the guidance of the Earl and Countess. “This we know because 
in September, 1576, only four days before his death, in Dublin, 
of a sudden attack of dysentery, the Earl of Essex had written : 
“My generall requeast to you all is to be lovinge to my Children 
and not to greve yourselfs wth the discontented newes of my 
death. Provide that my L. of Huntingdon be duly paid of the 
porcions appointed yerely for my doughters and for Wat my boy. 
I have assigned them to his Lordship and to my Lady and do assure 
myself they will not refuse them.” 2° Young Walter Devereux 
was the brother of Robert, Earl of Essex, most famous of Elizabeth’s 
favourites, and the step-son of another almost equally well known, 
for young Walter’s mother, soon after the death of her husband, 
married handsome Robert Dudley, Earl of Leicester. 

Where and how Margaret Dakins acquired her ability to read 


6 DIARY OF WA DY Oss 


and write, keep accounts, sing, and play the “alpharion”’, her 


knowledge of household and estate management, of surgery 
and salves, all which her diary shows her to have practised, there 
exists no definite indication. She merely performed, in her daily 
life, the tasks which fell to the lot of the women of her class 
generally. Perhaps she was trained in them at home by her 
mother, or maybe later by the Countess of Huntingdon. “The 
record of her daily doings as a married woman reflects on every 
page her strong Puritan convictions, and these were, more than 
probably, due to her upbringing in the house of the Countess . ‘This 
lady was the daughter of John Dudley, Duke of Northumberland, 
whose rash a¢tions lost him his head in 1553, and unfortunately 
cut short the inoffensive youth of Lady Jane Grey and her 
husband. In that same year Northumberland’s daughter 
Catherine married Henry Hastings, Edward VI’s early com- 
panion. Later, Hastings developed strong Puritan leanings, 
and openly and deeply sympathized with the Huguenot struggle 
in France. In 1569 Elizabeth made him joint custodian, with 
the Earl of Shrewsbury, of Mary Queen of Scots, and, in 1572, 
he was appointed President of the Council of the North. It was 
doubtless during his term of office in York that he came into 
contact with the Dakins family. The following year Huntingdon 
sat on the trial for high treason of the Duke of Norfolk, and, some 
years after, being Lord Lieutenant of six of the northern counties, he 
was appointed one of a commission to inquire into the recusancy 
of certain of the gentry suspected of Roman Catholicism. 
Huntingdon himself had a strong claim to the Crown in succession 
to Elizabeth. 

‘The stewardship and bailiwick of the town of Leicester were 
hereditary in the Hastings family and Henry, who became Earl 
in 1561, was Lord Lieutenant also of that county and of Rutland. 
He bought a residence in Leicester in the Swines market or High 
Street, and purchased in addition the site of the abbey of St. Mary, 
building himself a house out of the materials of the ruin. “The 
earl constantly evinced his interest in the town, where the majority 
thoroughly approved his strong Puritan leanings. He is said 
to have given money to found a le¢tureship there, and he certainly 
lent financial aid to start a workhouse where the poor could be 
taught weaving.*! “The town of Leicester, judging from frequent 
municipal ordinances, was as fond of sermons as Margaret Dakins 


PES Reve OPE Lae bony) Ft ©) By, 7 


later revealed herself to be. As early as 1582 the Mayor and his 
brethren ordered “that there be of euery howse one at euery 
sarmon vpon Wenday and Freydaye, apon pean of euery howsolder 
makyng defalt to forfytt iiid.”.?? In 1590 the stipend of their 
preacher, Thomas Sacheverell, was raised, at the earnest request 
of the Earl of Huntingdon, from £20 to £30 per annum.” Many 
were the presents made to the Countess of Huntingdon, not 
only by Mr. Mayor and his brethren, but by Mistress Mayoress 
and her sisters, and recorded in the town records. Camden 
wrote of the Earl that he was of a mild disposition, but ‘ being 
a zealous puritan, much wasted his estate by a lavish support 
of those hot-headed preachers’. “Che seventeenth century author 
of a manuscript history of the family put it still more strongly : 
“that he wasted his large Patrimony in noe small measure (and 
Probably upon those greedy Horse-leeches the Puritans) is certain 
enough ; as by the sale of divers Lordships and Lands upon easy 
pennyworths, . . . may in some sort appear.” 24 

The Earl died in 1595, but his Countess lived on, a childless 
woman, to a ripe old age. She was buried in August, 1620. 
Perhaps her ideal of what a woman should be was the same as the 
Earl’s, who once told the Earl of Rutland, at the latter’s marriage 
in 1573, “I trust you have chosen well, and I am sure of it if 
the report be true that she fears God, loves the Gospel, and hates 
Popery.” 25 There is extant a letter from the Countess to 
Sir Julius Czesar signed by her and dated as late as 1618. ‘The 
initial of her christian name is written with a great flourish, 
and in her own hand is a post-script, in small current Italic script. 
‘Thus she compliments herself: “... me, whoe though my self 
doe say it, I think ther will none make questen, but j} knoe how 
to breed and gouern yong gentlewomen.”?® Evidently the 
Countess was well-known for her ability in this direction. Another 
lady, one Mistress Webbe, who lived with her husband near 
Sir Matthew Arundel’s house at Shafton, was said to be, in 1597, 
“a woman well bred under the Countess of Huntingdon.” 27 
It would appear, then, that in this household Margaret Dakins 
would receive the best of trainings, nor would her sojourn there 
leave anything to be desired socially, for the Earl’s office of 
President of the Council of the North would give him, in the 
estimation of the country north of the Trent, by far the most 
influential and important position in that region. 


8 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


There is nothing to indicate how old Margaret Dakins was 
when she was placed in the care of the Countess of Huntingdon, 
so that her debt to the stern Puritan atmosphere of that household 
can only be a matter of conjecture. Her own home may have 
been as strictly and sincerely pious, while other and later influences 
must also have played their part in making her the woman her 
diary reveals her to have been—an accomplished housewife, an 
intelligent woman of affairs, and one with strong religious 
impulses. 

The up-bringing of girls was a matter of great importance 
in the economic organization of the country at this period in the 
history of its development. Lady Hoby’s diary excellently 
illustrates the results achieved by the methods then in vogue 
of training one se¢tion at least of the female half of the population. 
It should be borne in mind that in the sixteenth century the 
organization of society was still based upon the family, and, 
consequently, that a woman’s services were heavy and varied, 
certain of her duties being regarded as specially and peculiarly 
suited to her. No artificial obstacle was there to hinder her 
from their accomplishment: the state was not yet mechanical. 
In every walk of life women shouldered burdens every whit 
as heavy as those borne by the men. Let us take, for example, 
the complex duties of the women of the landed gentry, to which 
class the writer of the diary belonged. Not seldom it fell to the 
lot of these ladies to manage a large number of servants, to 
supervise the working of estates of which they were often left 
in charge, to ensure a store of food and clothing sufficient for the 
needs of a very numerous household, to understand the making 
of drugs and simples, and the pra¢tice of elementary surgery and 
midwifery. “hus, in spite of the somewhat restricted sphere of 
action of the Tudor lady, she yet had scope within it for the 
development of every capacity, mental, moral, and physical.28 If 
she lived in the country, where the village was still largely self- 
contained, she could, and often did, take an active part in the 
formation of public opinion and the life of the little community. 
Much of this last, on Lady Hoby’s part, was done, apparently, 
on Sundays. 

What we can glean of the life of Margaret Dakins, before she 
became Lady Hoby, is typical of the period. Before May, 1589, 
this girl, baptised in 1571, had married Walter Devereux. He 


DIARY OF LADY’ HOBY 9 


had been educated at Christ Church, Oxford, entering the 
University in June, 1584, at the age of fifteen. Early in 1588 
negotiations had commenced for the match, and the vexed question 
of settlements was under discussion. It was agreed, finally, 
that the manor and parsonage of Hackness should be purchased 
for the young couple for the sum of £6,500, of which Margaret’s 
father was to pay £3,000, the Earl of Essex for his brother, £3,000, 
and the Earl of Huntingdon, £500. ‘The sale was then completed 
through trustees, but, because of certain unfulfilled conditions, 79 
it was not until 1601, and after a prolonged law-suit, that the 
estate was conveyed wholly to the possession of Margaret and 
her husband. As long before as 1590, the Earl of Huntingdon 
had warned Devereux “to call in tyme for the assurance of 
Hackenes”’.3® Poor Margaret would have been saved much 
worry had her young husband aéted upon this advice of his father’s 
cousin. ‘The property was widespread and valuable: “the 
Manor of Hacknes als. Hacknesse, and 200 messuages and 4 mills 
with lands there and in Silpho, Suffielde, Everle, Hacknes Dale, 
Harewoode Dale, Brexay, Burneston, Huton Bushell, and Ayton, 
also the rectory and advowson of the vicarage of Hacknes als. 
Hacknesse church.” 34 

There seems to be few contemporary allusions to Margaret’s 
married life at Hackness with Walter Devereux, “that diamond 
of the time, and both of an hardy and delicate temper and 
mixture.” 92 The match was probably a wholly business 
transaction, such as was customary in Tudor England, though the 
contracting parties, aged twenty and eighteen, were somewhat 
older than usual. It was declared later, in the Court of Chancery, 
that the “ marryage was effected by the meanes of the sayde 
Earle of Essex and the late Earle of Huntingdon’. Furthermore, 
after young Devereux’s death in 1591 without issue, “ the said late 
Erle of Huntingdon procured the same Dame Margarett to be 
secondly marryed to his wyef’s nephewe, Tho. Sydney, whose 
preferment the same Earle sought.” *8 

It was characteristic of the period that social, financial, even 
political considerations preponderated in marriages arranged by 
parents or guardians for children who were too young, or too 
economically helpless, to grasp or grapple with the situation. 
‘The history of Margaret Dakins’s three marriages, for her first 
two husbands did not long survive, and she was only twenty-five 


10 DIARY VOPR CURA DY wituie 


when she took her third and last spouse, has nothing to relieve 
it of the purely material, for she was the only child of wealthy 
parents, and a highly desirable partze for the poverty-stricken 
younger sons of the older families. It is not possible to agree 
with the opinion that “if anything of Margaret can be learned 
from these three marriages it is that she could not bear living 
alone, and that she was evidently attractive ”.34 Margaret had 
no voice in the choosing of her three husbands, nor did she reveal, 
as far as we know, any eagerness for marriage. We should 
remember, however, before condemning the apparent selfishness 
or hard-headed ambition of the Tudor parent, that the unmarried 
woman of the time, left without protection, was in a peculiarly 
helpless position. Furthermore, if she were wealthy, or an 
heiress, she was the prey of every undesirable and poverty- 
stricken trickster who came along. 

Margaret’s diary must have passed into, and remained in, the 
possession of her third husband’s nephew, John Sydenham and 
his heirs, to whom the estate of Hackness was bequeathed in 
1640, and from one of whose descendants the diary came finally 
into the keeping of the British Museum. But not so the letters 
and papers which relate to Margaret’s courtships and marriages, 
particularly to the third and last one. “These, with other papers 
of a less private nature, passed to the purchaser of Hackness from 
the Sydenhams. This was Mr. John van den Bempde who 
bought the property in 1707. For a long period, from the death 
of Margaret’s third husband, who outlived her, until the year 
1792 when Sir Richard van den Bempde Johnstone took possession 
and began to build a new hall, the estate seems never to have had 
a resident squire, and mortgage after mortgage testifies to the 
dilapidation which finally resulted in the sale of Hackness by the 
Sydenhams.®® 

Margaret’s married life with Walter Devereux may have 
been unhappy, for his behaviour was remembered by the neigh- 
bouring gentry. When young William Eure, protesting against 
the churlish reception of himself and his hunting party at Hackness 
manor in August, 1600, showed resentment on departure at his 
treatment, he emphasized his annoyance by “ throwing stones 
at the windows and breaking quarrels of glass”, declaring, at the 
same time, that he “ would go to the top of the hill and fling 
down millstones and would play young Devereux ”.36 Walter’s 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY II 


father had died when the lad was only ten, thus having little 
opportunity to create again in this second son his own quiet love 
for “‘ chronicles, histories, genealogies, and pedigrees of noble men 
and noble houses not only within the realm, but also in foreign 
realms”. “This Earl of Essex, Strype tells us, excelled “in 
describing and blazoning of arms, and in all skill pertaining 
thereto ”’.37. Whatever Walter was, and whatever the view of 
him conceived by his Yorkshire neighbours, Margaret was not 
troubled with him long. His brother Robert was sent by Elizabeth 
to assist the Protestant Henry IV of France in 1591, in thesiege 
of Rouen, and Walter, going in his train as leader of the horse,?® 
was killed in the first skirmish. Essex loved his brother dearly : 
as he told Sir Robert Cecil, he ““ was dearer to me than ever I was 
to myself’, and he declared to Lord Burghley that he had “ lost 
in this aétion him, who, next herself [the Queen] was dearest 
to me of all the world”’.8® Indeed, the whole army mourned 
the loss of so worthy a gentleman.?° 

From now on, a little more is known of the background of 
Margaret’s life, thanks to many private letters which concern 
her courtships and marriages.44 No sooner was she a widow 
than, within a fortnight of her husband’s death, the hive of match- 
makers began once more to buzz. ‘This time there were two 
competing hives, both well-known, both influential: that of 
the Hastings family on the one hand, and that of the Cecils on 
the other. Now the Countess of Huntingdon had Strategically 
the best position, for she was Margaret’s old mistress, and her 
candidate, in the person of her own favourite nephew and 
husband’s ward, young “Thomas Sidney, was ready and 
waiting. The Countess of Huntingdon and Lady Sidney, 
the mother of ‘Thomas and his famous brother, Philip, and their 
no less well-known sister, Mary, were sisters, both being daughters 
of John Dudley, Earl of Northumberland. It may even be that 
Margaret had all along cared for ‘Thomas, for, as children, they 
must have spent some time together in the Earl’s household : 
‘Thomas Sidney, left a minor at his father’s death in 1586, was 
placed by him under the guardianship of his uncle, the Earl of 
Huntingdon.*2 ‘To Mr. Dakinsin Yorkshire the Earl sent young 
Sidney post haste with letters, as soon as the news of Walter 
Devereux’s death reached England. By these, Thomas Sidney 
“ prevayled soe fare with owlde Mr. Dakyns .. . as that he sent

his daughter to London forthwith, unto my La. of Huntyngdone,

Margaret assuredly left her home and parents, and departed 
for London on 2nd November, 1591. Her husband had been 
killed on 8th September. Events moved fast, for on 22nd 
December, 1591, the Bishop of London granted a licence for the 
marriage of “Thomas Sydney of All Hallows in the Wall, London, 
Esq., and Margaret Devereux of same, widow of Walter Devereux, 
Esq., late of Hacknesse, co. York.” 44 Perhaps not even so long 
a time as some fourteen weeks would have elapsed between her 
husband’s death and Margaret’s re-marriage had not young 
Devereux’s friends rescued his body and placed it in a lead coffin, 
intending to carry it triumphantly into Rouen at the end of the 
siege.45 “This caused delay in burial, and the etiquette of the 
time forbade the widow’s re-marriage until after the funeral of her 
late husband. Margaret, a member already of the Devereux 
family, now entered the Sidney circle, becoming linked, through 
her mother-in-law, still more closely with the Earldom of 
Huntingdon and the Hastings conneétion, and also with the 
Earldom of Bedford and the Russell family, for Lady Sidney’s 
brother, Ambrose, Earl of Warwick, had married Anne Russell, 
daughter of the Earl of Bedford. 

And so the hopes of the other suitor—or fortune hunter— 
were dashed. ‘This unlucky fellow was a serious young man 
of twenty-five, Thomas Posthumous Hoby, second son of Sir 
‘Thomas Hoby of Bisham, Berkshire. Sir “Thomas had died 
in Paris in 1566 in the midst of his work as Elizabeth’s ambassador 
to France, and just before the birth of his second son.4® His 
wife was Elizabeth, one of the five well-educated daughters of 
Sir Anthony Cooke, of Gidea Hall, Essex, whom he had married 
when she was thirty years old. Elizabeth’s sister, Mildred, 
had become the second wife of William Cecil, afterwards Lord 
Burghley, England’s wily Lord Treasurer, while another of this 
brilliant family of daughters, Ann, married Sir Nicholas Bacon 
and so was the mother of that famous philosopher and lawyer, 
Francis. Six years after the death of her first husband, Lady 
Elizabeth Hoby re-married, in 1574, at the age of forty-six. 
Her second husband was John, Lord Russell, the son of Francis

Earl of Bedford, and by him she had two daughters, Elizabeth 
and Ann. ‘Through this marriage Lady Hoby created, for the 
two sons of her first union, Edward and ‘Thomas, a second set 
of influential connections. Related on the one hand to the 
Cecils by Mildred Cooke’s marriage to Burghley, they found, 
through their step-father, new female relatives who again widened 
the field of their relations. “The Earl of Bedford’s daughter, 
Margaret Russell, later wedded that bold buccaneer, George 
Earl of Cumberland, and so became the mother of Anne, Lady 
Clifford. ‘To Anne Russell, who married Ambrose Earl of 
Warwick, we have already referred. “This lady was very intimate 
with the Queen, deep in her confidence, and a person of great 
importance at the Court, a force and influence to be reckoned 
with by anyone who would approach the throne, or dabble in 
affairs of state. 

Elizabeth Lady Russell was an outstanding personality, in an 
age which, in many respects, encouraged the growth of individuality. 
In her, a mere brief notice of her qualities would have created 
resentful and outspoken indignation. ‘This lady, the mother of 
‘Thomas Posthumous Hoby, is one of the most interesting women 
in the Tudor period. Her father was a scholar of some repute 
who had helped to educate Edward VI, and, under his tuition, 
Elizabeth and her sisters became some of the most learned women 
in the country. He bent all his energies to the education of his 
children, and himself taught his five little maids, for he believed, 
as did not a few of the wise men of that time, that “ sexes as well 
as souls are equal in capacity ”’.47 His household fitly compares 
with that of Sir Thomas More. His panegyrist, Lloyd, declared 
that Sir Antony Cooke’s first anxiety for his family was “to 
embue their infancy with a knowing, serious, and sober religion, 
which went with them to their graves; and his next, to inure 
their youth to obedience and modesty ”. In addition he cultivated 
their talents till they were renowned for learning, and yet taught 
them, at the same time, to regard all study as a recreation, and 
to “place their real business in the needle in the closet, and 
housewifery in kitchen and hall”. Under his love, and the 
authority which his character and precepts gave him, these girls 
came near to fulfilling, in their lives, their father’s great purpose, 
which was “that his daughters might have for their husbands 
complete or perfect men, and that their husbands might be happy 


14 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


in complete women”.48 In his will, Sir Anthony left some 
of his books to his daughters. Mildred was to have two volumes 
in Latin and one in Greek, and to make her own choice: Lady 
Russell and Lady Killigrew were to have the same.” 

No matter whence it came, from the tuition of her father in 
religion and the classics, or from the practical teaching of life 
itself, Lady Russell developed astounding force of character, 
as her letters abundantly testify. Many of these are stil 
fortunately preserved amongst the papers at Hatfield House 
and in the Lansdowne colleétion at the British Museum. ‘The 
lengthy epitaphs in Greek and Latin verse on the family tombs 
in Bisham Church and Westminster Abbey, which were written 
by her, and her translation of a treatise from the French, printed 
in 1605, and dedicated to her daughter in an address which ends 
in a Latin verse, sufficiently prove her linguistic attainments.°® 
Her well-informed and lengthy epistles, with their frequent 
Latin tags, their wit, and their sarcasm, her actions and law-suits 
on behalf of her daughters’ inheritance and for the upkeep of 
her own dignity, the knowledge she exhibited of legal practice 
and estate management, all bear witness to her intellectual ability 
and domineering imperiousness.°! 

The well-read Queen herself commended her in the letter 
of condolence she sent in September, 1566, on the death of her 
first husband: ‘‘ And for your self we cannot but let you know 
that we hear out of France such singular good reports of your 
duty well accomplished towards your husband, both living and 
dead, with other your sober, wise, and discreet behaviour in that 
Court and Country, that we think it a part of great contentation 
to us, and commendation of our Country, that such a Gentle- 
woman hath given so manifest a testimony of virtue in such hard 
times of adversity. And therefore though we thought very well 
of you before, yet shall we hereafter make a more assured account 
of your virtues and gifts, and wherein soever we may conveniantly 
do you pleasure, you may be thereof assured. And so we would 
have you to rest yourself in quietness, with a firm opinion of our 
especiall favour towards you.’ 52 Holbein considered her 
features worthy of preservation since he drew her picture. Yet 
Shakespeare probably disliked her intensely because of a petition 
which she, with Lord Hunsdon and twenty-nine other inhabitants 
of the Blackfriars, presented to the Privy Council in 1596. The 


De eye (Ora A Ys TL OB Yi 15 


complaint of these puritanical Londoners stated that one Burbage 
had lately bought rooms near Lord Hunsdon’s, and was converting 
them into a common playhouse, which would be a great annoyance 
to the neighbourhood, because of the gathering of vagrant and 
lewd persons, on pretence of coming to the plays; because of 
its making the place too populous, in case of a return of the sick- 
ness; and because, the playhouse being near the church, the 
drums and trumpets would disturb divine service. “They declared 
that there had not before been any playhouse in the precinéts, 
but now that the Lord Mayor had banished the players from the 
city, they planted themselves in the liberties. “Therefore they 
requested that no playhouse be kept there.®® 

As this redoubtable lady grew older, and especially after she 
became a widow for the second time, on the death of Lord John 
Russell, her actions proved that recourse to law held no terrors 
for her. She was one of those people upon whom Dekker com- 
mented satirically in The Dead Terme: ‘‘ Had not the people 
of this large Kingdome faine to so many priuate quarrels, about 
bloues giuen ; ‘Io so many intricate bargains, about buying and 
selling of lands; “To so many Cozenages of wicked Executors, in 
vndoing Orphans and Heires, and so many starting holes, Crannies, 
Creuises, windings, wrestlings, rackings, Circumgirations, and 
Circumuentions, to abuse the beautiful body of the Law, and to 
make it grow crooked, which of itselfe is comly and vpright;... 
Had not the Inhabitants of this Empire warred so in law from 
time to time, one with another, shee coulde not haue boasted 
of so many Graue, wise, and Learned Iudges. Of so many 
Discreet, Sage, and reuerend Iustices, Of so many carefull, 
prudent, and honourable Maiestrates.”’ °4 

Lady Russell actually petitioned the Privy Council in 
October, 1593, concerning the punishment of her neighbour 
Mr. Lovelace, to whom, she declared, she had shown every 
friendship these twenty-six years, but who had, it appeared, 
been guilty of foul riots against her. On Monday, 1st October, 
1593, Lovelace had come to her house with sixteen or twenty 
men bearing halberds and long poles, had broken open her 
porter’s lodge, and the lock of her stocks, and taken out two of 
his own men who had behaved lewdly to her, and so had been 
placed there by her orders. If, declared Lady Russell, she had 


offered him or his any wrong, the law was “ open ’”’, and it was 


16 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


not for him, a justice of peace, to break his oath by so foul a riot. 
Therefore she desired that he be put out of the commission of 
the peace. Further, Lady Russell complained, upon the ‘Thursday 
following, Lovelace had sent a man for the key of the “Tower 
at Windsor, where she had been all the year, having all her stuff 
there. She refused to leave upon such sudden warning, unless 
by order of the Lord Admiral, and offered him as much rent 
as it was worth, but it was refused. “The Saturday following, 
he and his men changed the lock of her lodging, and commanded 
that none should undo it. She desired that this spiteful injury 
might be punished, and her goods “answered”; otherwise it 
were better to be “‘ a mean justice of the peace than a noble woman 
that depended upon God and Her Majesty ”’.5> ‘The advice 
of Sir Edward Coke, the Attorney-General, to Sir Robert Cecil, 
Lady Russell’s nephew and Elizabeth’s Secretary of State, upon 
the matter, is both interesting and significant. “I have con- 
sidered of the state of the cause between my lady Russell and 
Mr. Lovelace, and of the proceedings on either part, and I take 
it the Star Chamber is no fit Court for my lady to complain in ; 
for, as your honour knoweth, that high court without respect 
striketh on both sides, and, in this case, the causes be so intermixed 
as on the one day they cannot punish Lovelace but on the other 
they must sentence against my lady. For, albeit an honourable 
lady being so abused as she was could hardly (all circumstances 
considered) brook such indignities, yet her stocking and imprison- 
ment of his men is not justifiable in law, and seeing there is so 
great inequality of persons, I would not have them suffer equal 
punishment. But, if it would please you and other of the 
honourable lords of her Highness’s Council to call Lovelace 
before you and let him understand the quality of his offence and, 
if he do not to my lady Russell’s satisfaction submit himself, 
that then it would please you to bind him over till the matter might 
be more deeply examined, in my opinion it were the best and 
safest course for my lady.” 5® In a later letter, upon a different 
matter, Lady Russell complained to her nephew that she thought 
a lady of her position was ‘‘ no way subje¢t to any justice of peace’s 
letter ”’.57 Undoubtedly it was good in Tudor England to be 
of the aristocracy, and, still more, to have friends at Court ! 

It was, however, in her old age that Lady Russell figured as 
the heroine in a real cause célébre, one to which Shakespeare

perhaps alludes in The Merry Wives of Windsor. In 1590

he found he could not, by any possible means, eject the Lady 
Russell, who opposed her right of custody to his of ownership. 
Two years went by. “The Queen died. ‘Then on 11th

the waie of Neweberrye, hath recovered the possession of

my Lady Russell, and kepethe the Castell and her ladyship owt 
of dores ; who complayned to the King, but found littell redress, 
and so is turned to the Lawe.”’ 58 

On her return to Donington from Wales, Lady Russell had, 
indeed, found the Lord Admiral’s men in possession. Refused 
admission, she sat in her coach outside the gates, and, nothing 
daunted, sent for the nearest Justice of Peace, desiring him to 
restore her possession, commit the so-called rioters, and bind the 
rest to peace. “This the bold J.P. surprisingly refused to do, 
being not at all sure of Lady Russell’s rights. “Thereupon the 
Lord Admiral’s lieutenant approached to tell the lady that, if 
she would acknowledge that she held the castle only by permission

the end, Lady Russell lost her case and was ordered to pay costs, 
all the judges “‘ condemninge greatelye the pryde and wyllfullnes 
of the plaintiff’.6® It should be remembered that when 
Lady Russell endeavoured thus to conduct her own case in the 
Court of Star Chamber, she was 78 years of age. 

She was a woman of manifold pursuits and interests. Most 
of her letters which have survived were written with her own 
hand. She was a prolific correspondent and seems to have seized 
her pen on the slightest excuse. One letter, the probable date 
of which is 1596, reveals her interest in the affairs of government. 
It was sent to her nephew, Sir Robert Cecil. ‘* Yield your best 
favour to a godly, honest and honourable nobleman, the Earl of 
Kent, to be in the Earl of Huntingdon’s place. It is thought 
there cannot be a fitter supply for the good of God’s church and 
that country, to Her Majesty’s service. I think my sister of 
Warwick will thank you heartily for your favour toward him. 
I would not have it known to proceed from me, because he is a 
widower and I a widow, but being entreated thereto, I could not 
do but thus much in respect of my duty to my dead. I beseech you 
quod facis fac cito, or else I fear one of the tribe will be before 
him Hercules Furens. ‘Thus not well able to write more, I end 
with I pray take my thanks for the five pendants to Bess [her 
daughter] at Newyearstide, with the remembrance of the five 
wonds in Ireland. And so, I beseech you, let me end with I pray 
forget not Mr. Dale, whom your mother and my lord your father 
preferred to be Master of the Requests, when Roockeby was 
preferred thereto by Mr. Walsingham. So wishing yourself 
Chancellor of the Duchy or Secretary, or both, with this beginning 
of the new year, I take my leave.” © A few months later Lady 


DIARY (OR LADY HOBBY 21 


Russell endeavoured again to use her influence on behalf of 
Mr. Dale: “ Sir Robert Cecill, this night I hear that Mr. Rookby 
is dead. If you will get yourself creditt, do your best to place 
Mr. Dale for, a temporal lawyer, a master of the Requests; for 
want whereof there is exceeding want and complaint by the 
subjects, poor suitors, and this man very much desired of my 
lord chief Baron and the rest of the chief judges, as a most sufficient 
man for that place, being so learned, grave, and of such experience. 
Therefore, since his white hairs show him to be so ancient a 
lawyer, so wise men think him most fit, and himself cometh to 
you in your mother’s name, macta virtute, with God’s blessing. 
And let not Doctor CHa a civilian, deprive him of the fee, 
due by patent to a temporal lawyer, and not to a civilian, who, 
besides, hath enough already, if these days could acknowledge 
what is enough. So, loath to trouble you further at this time, 
I humbly thank God for the heavenly breath proceeding from a 
saint so sweet and gracious to me as you write.” ® 

In 1597 Lady Russell was busy match-making for her daughters: 
she had two, Elizabeth and Ann, by her second husband. She 
requested Secretary Cecil to try to arrange a marriage for the 
eldest, already twenty-two. This girl was not only Maid of 
Honour to the Queen, but her god-daughter also. Wrote Lady 
Russell : “ This is all I have to trouble you with, but desire you 
in this being of the Earl of Worcester’s daily in Court, it will 
please you in your best opportunity to persuade the Earl so as my 
daughter Bess may be wife to Lord Harbart his eldest son. Her 
vertue, birth, and place, joined to the hundred pounds of 
inheritance presently enjoyed and the part in reversion of my 
Lady Gray, joined with two hundred pounds yearly after my 
death till two thousand pounds be come out in ten years to her 
own good whether she be sole or married, will be a sufficient 
portion for an Earl of so small revenue and so many children as 
the Earl of Worcester. It is the virtue and honour of the 
parents joined with the young lord’s best affections that 
maketh me thus desirous. Else I seek it not. Your loving 
Aunt, Elizabeth Russel, Dowager.” §* On 24th June, 1600, 
John Chamberlain wrote to Mr. Carleton: “I doubt not but 
you have heard of the great marriage at the Lady Russell’s, 
where the Quene was present, being caried from the water-side 
in a curious chaire and lodged at the Lord Cobhams ; and of the 


22 DIARY. OF LADY HOBBY 


maske of eight maides of honour and other gentlewomen in name of 
the Muses that came to seeke one of their fellowes.” ® It was, 
however, Ann, the younger girl, who on this great occasion 
became Lady Herbert. Her sister Elizabeth died unmarried in 
the following year, and was buried in Westminster Abbey.®® 

Several members of the influential families with which Lady 
Russell was connected were keen supporters of Puritanism, 
and intelligent students of the religious controversies of the day. 
To Thomas Cartwright, who had been deprived of his Chair 
and Fellowship at Cambridge on account of his opinions, most 
of their circle seems to have been personally known. ‘There is 
extant a long letter written to Lady Russell while he was in prison 
inthe Fleet, in August, 1591, describing hissufferings, and obviously 
replying to a letter from her which must have contained an offer 
of help. He refers to the kind treatment he had received at her 
hands, and to the time when she, presenting him to her two 
daughters, had commended him as a man “whom for good 
respects” she favoured. In this letter Cartwright puts his defence 
before Lady Russell, leaving to her own discretion what she 
shall say in her promised suit to Lord Burghley on his _ behalf. 
Lady Russell evidently forwarded the letter, as it was, to her 
brother-in-law, since it has been preserved amongst his papers, 
but endorsed it, ““God my Lord rede this thorow and do what 
good yow can to the poore man.” ®7 

By her very nature, Lady Russell was bound to be a strong force 
in her children’s lives. Ever wide awake to all hazards, on 
7th April, 1566, when about to sail for France with her first 
husband the ambassador, she wrote a farewell letter to Sir William 
Cecil from Dover, demanding in the post-script : “‘ Send me word 
whether any child born beyond sea shall inherit land in England, 
though I yet have no cause to demand it.” 8 However, leaving 
nothing to chance, she so arranged matters that her son, Thomas 
Posthumous, Margaret Dakins’s suitor and third husband, was 
born in England. ‘Thomas was, apparently, an undersized 
difficult child, who gave his mother a vast deal of trouble. Once 
he ran away from home, an occurrence which called forth from 
her a long and indignant epistle which she addressed to Lord 
Burghley : “I fownd my Chyld in Sheppey, and he is now 
here. “Che cawse of his departure to be, bicawse he shoold this 
next tearme by my appoyntment have ben placed in Innes of

Coorte, for his better instruction: where I had taken order, 
he shoold have ben placed at michaelmas last was a twelvemoneth, 
as Mr. Farmer can witness wt me ;_ but that, in respect of his 
litleness, Mr. Farmer entreated me, both in respect of his owne 
creditt, and ye howse, I wold forbeare till he were bigger, for yt 
he shoold be reputed as a Chyld. “The Boy sayth yt by no meanes 
he can frame him self to like or to take that Coorse, to his owne

vncertayne, frivolows ; the languages to be learned, wt the Scite

him, procured first of myne owne inclination, wtowt ye perswasion 
of eny living or ded. . . . Neather shall eny Living, I except 
none, perswade me, during myne owne naturall lyfe, to assure 
eny more to him, sins he had ye face to say to my self yt, if yt 
had ben of my doing, it shoold not have ben so assured to him. 


My Lord, thogh I be not so bad a bird as to defyle myne owne

nest, yet I know my Children, as well as ye wisest shall, in tyme,

Nor, continued this masterful parent, had she been “ careless to

vnderstoode ye lest of mislike or want before his letter, sent after 
his departure. His Brother sweareth and avoweth yt he knew 
no thing of his folly, but submitteth him self and his service in 
most dutyfull manner.” 74 

Such was the mother, and such the man, who laid siege to 
Margaret Devereux’s fortune in the autumn of 1591—* the 
little Knight,” as he became in 1594, “that useth to draw up 
his Breeches with a shooing-horn.” 7 Less than a fortnight 
after Devereux’s death Lord Burghley himself opened the court- 
ship for his nephew by a letter to the Earl of Huntingdon, written 
on 21st September, 1591, in which he Joined with his sister-in-law,

Lady Russell, on behalf of “my honest servante and nephewe

Hoby, who had no children, and that he was therefore likely to 
inherit his brother’s portion as well as his own. In doing so, he, 
perhaps inadvertently, revealed Lady Russell’s influence: “his 
mother hath provyded a good portione of lyvelod to be left to 
hym, yf he shall content her in his marryadge, and wyll deal very 
honorably and kyndly with hym, to enhable hym to make to his 
wyfe a convenient joynture, in case she shall lyke of his choyse.” 
With a little judicious flattery concerning Margaret’s “ vertues

and conditiones soe reported to us very credybly”’, Burghley 
concluded by requesting from Dakins a speedy answer, 
alleging as his reason, ‘‘ soe as the party may have comforte to

for his nephew first place in the race for the hand of the widowed

Immediately after the receipt of this letter, on roth November, 
Mr. Dakins wrote a most humble and apologetic reply to the all- 
powerful Lord Treasurer, stating that a week before, as we already 
know, Margaret had departed for London with letters to the Earl 
of Huntingdon and the Countess, her old mistress, “ yeeldynge 
therby my consente to theyr honors for the disposynge of my 
daughter in her maryadge, which God knoweth is meane, and 
farr unworthy the proffer your Ho. doth make by your sayde 
lettres.” He proceeded further to profess his deep regret that he 
could not grant Burghley’s request, which “by any meanes I 
Stande moste bownd soe to doe for your former goodness towards 
me, the which as your Honour doth not remember, soe shall I 
never forget them.” 75 Dakins’s attitude to Burghley, as expressed 
in this letter, is sufficient to prove what need there was for haste 
and secrecy on the part of the Huntingdons if they wished to secure 
Margaret for their nephew, ‘Thomas Sidney. Besides desiring to 
advance one in his own service and the son of the redoubtable 
Lady Russell, Lord Burghley may also have wanted at Hackness 
a man on whom he could rely, for it was situated in the centre 
of a population holding firmly to the creed and church of their 
ancestors: landowner and peasant alike protected the lurking 
priests and united to resist by force attempts to coerce them into 
religious conformity.”® A sparsely-peopled, hilly, well-wooded 
distri€t, close to the coast, it lent itself admirably to such 
practices. 

Neither Hoby nor his mother gave up hope, even when it was 
known that Margaret had left Hackness. Lady Russell had long 
before approached the Earl of Essex, Margaret’s brother-in-law, 
and now her son sought the help of Lady Perrot, Essex’s sister, 
and from her gathered the information, on 13th November, that 
the gentillwomane you know of is come to my Lorde of Hunt- 
ingdon’s”’. Meanwhile his energetic mother was writing to

mother, not as a suitor till the funeral be past.” 8! It is devoutly 
to be hoped that Margaret was “in affection gon to Sidney ”, 
for nothing apparently could, or did, prevent her marriage to him. 

Margaret’s father, Arthur Dakins, died in the following year at 
Hackness, on 13th July, 1592, at the age of seventy-five, and there 
he was buried. His will, made in May, 1589,* left to his wife a life 
estate in his manors of Lintonand Thirkleby, in the tithe of corn and 
grain in the town and fields of Place Newton and ‘Thirkleby, 
and in all his property in the parishes of Kirkby “ Grindallieth ” 
and Wintringham, which he had bought from George Dakyns, 
late of Foulbridge, with remainder to his daughter Margaret, 
then wife to Walter Devereux, Esq., in tail, with remainders 
in tail male to his brother Humphrey of Chelmorton in the Peak, 
and tohisnephews. After enumerating certain bequests of money, 
he bequeathed the residue to his wife and daughter, whom he 
made his executors. Dakins’s monument in Hackness church, 
a slab of white marble imposed on black, was erected to his memory 
by Margaret and her husband in 1597. It remains to-day on 
the north wall of the chancel within the altar rails, its inscription, 


28 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


accompanied by a wealth of heraldry, setting forth in detail the 
family of Margaret’s mother, and of her own three husbands. *8 

On 26th July, 1595, Margaret became a widow for the second 
time. “Thomas Sidney was buried at Kingston-upon-Hull, 
in which place he apparently owned some property, for the manor 
of Kingston became hers upon his death : and we hear, later, of 
Mistress Sidney’s selling there ‘a howse and a small quantyty of 
land”? to the best advantage, and the proceeds going to pay the 
debts of Mr. Thomas Sidney.*4 

Once more Margaret was a matrimonial prize to be striven for, 
and this time Thomas Posthumous Hoby, now a knight, lost 
no time. His first failure had witnessed a renewal of the dis- 
agreeable relations between him and his mother ;_ he told Robert 
Cecil, in February, 1595, of her “‘ unkind strangeness ”’, and that 
he had not been admitted to see her since his return to England.®® 
The resumption of the undignified treasure-hunt once more 
united mother and son. Barely a week had Mistress Sidney been 
again a widow before Hoby delivered a letter concerning her to 
the old Earl of Huntingdon from his powerful uncle, the Lord 
Treasurer Burghley, and found the former “very honourably 
bent ” to show him favour. His mother too, as Hoby related to 
Cecil, was “* forwards 1n the cause, so as I have no reason to doubt of 
my good success, except I should be crossed by some prerogative 
suitor that shall obtain Her Majesty’s letters for his strength ; 
therefore my suit is you would use some means to cross them if 
any shall attempt those proceedings ’’.86 By 12th September, 
the Earl of Huntingdon had received letters both from Lord 
Burghley and Lady Russell on Hoby’s behalf, and had spoken 
with Hoby about his suit. “This time, however, for what reason 
we do not know, though the Earl was anxious to show Hoby 
favour, he was reluctant to meddle ; ‘so bad hath bin my successe 
that yf I might be spared I would never deale that way agayne 
for any such matter.”8? ‘This time he brought no pressure to 
bear on Margaret but merely wrote to Edward Stanhope, a member 
of his Council of the North, son of the late Sir Michael Stanhope, 
Governor of Kingston-upon-Hull, and related in some way or 
other to both parties, asking him, at Hoby’s request, “to 
accompany him to the place that he may the redilyar havea sight 
of the gentlewoman.” 88 

Ten days later, Stanhope and Hoby presented themselves 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 29 


at the manor house near Hull where Margaret was staying. 
They found her “layde complayninge of payne in her eyes and 
heade ”’, which proceeded “‘ of greate lamentacion for the losse 
of the worthy gentleman her late husbande, for she coulde not 
then speake of him without teares”. Stanhope alone was 
admitted to her presence, and gave to her Lord Huntingdon’s 
letters. Immediately she had read them and learned the cause of 
Stanhope’s coming, she “‘ shedd teares againe, sayinge that thoughe 
she helde her selfe bounde to your Lordship to whom she was 
wholly devoted, yet the tender love she bare to him that was dead, 
made yt grevous to her to hear of any newe ’’, and she proceeded to 
tell Stanhope what she felt for a suitor who could trouble her at 
such a time. Nevertheless, after listening to many apologies 
for intrusion, much cajolery and flattery, and protestations of 
Hoby’s sincerity and eagerness, Margaret unwillingly consented 
to speak, for a little while, with her eager would-belover. She kept 
him waiting for an hour or more, and then he was admitted to 
her presence, and saw her, in person, for the first time: “ where 
curteousley and modestly intertayning him with fewe speeches, she 
retired to the gentlewomen, and, after smal tyme spent in the 
chamber,”’ Stanhope and Hoby left her—to convert Margaret’s 
hosts into allies, and discuss with them a plan of campaign for the 
courtship.®® Hoby was all in favour of immediate persistent 
prosecution of his suit, but it was decided that it were better if 
he waited for the Earl of Huntingdon’s return to the north before 
again presenting himself, and that he should come the second time 
in the Earl’s own influential company, if possible, to show that 
his intentions were serious. It was then agreed that he should 
leave the distri€t and go south for a time, pretending tender regard 
for Mistress Sidney’s reputation. Hoby hypocritically protested 
that his chief desire for the match was “in regard of the gentle- 
woeman’s vertues, whereof he heareth by all that speake of her ’’.9° 
The great Burghley himself found time to write later, in the midst 
of his attacks of gout, personal letters to Stanhope and to Margaret’s 
host, to thank them for their assistance to his nephew. 

But the young widow, seemingly, was not attracted. Perhaps 
Hoby’s appearance was against him. His mother had, some 
years before, shown herself fully aware of the drawback of his 
want of stature: his enemies, a few years later, hurled at 
him the epithets “‘ scurvy urchen ” and “ spindle shanked ape ”’.9*

Towards the end of Oétober, Hoby was busy collecting letters 


in his own favour from all his influential friends **—anything 
to strengthen his suit—and Sir Robert Cecil, on 28th Odtober, 
wrote to the Earl of Huntingdon, at Hoby’s request, to thank 
him for the favour already shown towards his cousin, and beg its 
continuance, which he would regard as a service to himself.%% 
The following day Hoby announced his impending return to the 
north, and begged for Cecil’s further help, telling him his lady 
mother was now so backward for his preferment that he had no 
‘* other hope to draw her to do anything but by the Lord ‘T’reasurer’s 
means ”’, which some of his friends were endeavouring to get for 
him.%4 

In November Hoby rode north again, this time with a “ good 
Store of faire Jewels and Pearls ’’,° and letters from Lord Burghley 
to the Earl of Huntingdon about the match. But Margaret was 
very cold and inaccessible to her persistent suitor, who would 
never have made any headway in his suit, had he not “ learned 
a former lessone of audaces fortuna jurat, wherby ”, he declared, 
‘“T ame ledd contynually to exceed good manners in beynge more 
ruled by my love then reasone ’’.°6 Poor Margaret herself wrote 
to the Earl to decline emphatically her very importunate lover, 
but the Earl’s kind reply of gth December gave her definitely to 
understand that Hoby refused to take her denial as final. “The 
conclusion of his letter is curious: ‘‘ For God’s cawse have care 
of all our credyts, and soe handle the matter as his commynge 
agayne may be neyther offensyve to you nor dyspleasynge to 
hymself.”” 9? Such wording seems to imply that, on religious 
grounds if on no other, the Earl, and Burghley too, commended 
Hoby to Margaret. Certain it is that he later proved himself a 
most uncompromising Protestant, well hated and distrusted by 
the Catholic community of the North Riding. 

Less than a week after his letter to Margaret, the old Earl of 
Huntingdon died, and fate played into Hoby’s hands, for the new 
Earl, his brother, at once laid claim to Margaret’s estate of Hackness 
on the ground that the purchase money, originally advanced by 
the trustees, had never been fully paid up by the other parties to 
the transaction, and that he was himself the real owner of the 
property °°; thus Mistress Sidney was faced with a law-suit 
in Chancery. Meanwhile Hoby continued to importune his 
powerful friends and connections for letters or representations

of some sort in his favour; his cousin, Anthony Bacon; the 
widowed Countess of Huntingdon, Margaret’s old mistress ; 
Sir Robert Sidney, her brother-in-law ; the Countess of Essex, 
her sister-in-law. ‘The last-named refused his request, “not 
but that I holde you worthe of har you desir, but that in honnor 
I cannot be for you sens I have promest an othar not to be aganst
