appointment to trye his credytt with my Lord ‘Treasurer for you, 
I know his Lordship may sway the matter wholly, and I am 
assured he so much affecteth his kinsman, as if he fynde that the 
mocion proceedeth from your self, and that Sir Thomas shall have 
kynde thanks of you for yt, he will stryke it sure for you. Herein 
use your owne discrecion, for if I were not assured that the speciall 
favour I wish you to afford Sir Thomas for his long service and 
entyer affeccion should not fall out as much to your good and 
comfort hereafter, as his, and that I know his estate shall be so well 
supplyed by his honorable mother as that he shall be able (without 
that which you bring) to maintaine you according to his degree, 
I protest to you, by the faith of an honest man, I would not use 
thes speeches unto you, or seeke any way to draw you to your 
hinderance, and knowing the trust you repose in me, which I will 
never deceyve; and therfore what I have ingaged my creditt 
unto you for, I doubt not but be able always to maintaine, and 
even so referring you to your owne good wisdome and honorable 
government, which hitherto you have caryed of yourself to your 
great creditt, I leave the report of the rest of the business to Mr. 
Mease, and so betake you to God.” 1°! What could a poor 
widow do, faced with a suit in Chancery where only influence 
would give her victory? Is it possible to place reliance upon 


32 DIARY: (OFULA DY GH ORY 


Stanhope’s statements concerning the Lord Keeper Egerton 
and the Lord Treasurer Burghley ? Margaret, at all events, 
believed him. On 26th June, Hoby wrote a fulsome letter of 
thanks to the Countess of Huntingdon for her good will in which 
he spoke of having been, hitherto, “‘ but a meer stranger, and soe 
have wanted meanes to merrytt your Honour’s favourable 
conceyte,”’ but that, once in possession of the happiness he now 
sought, he would “ be fownde as dutyfully servyceable, as if I wer 
a naturall branch of the stocke yt self, whereto I shall then be 
but grafted ”’.102 

On 1st August, 1596, Lady Russell issued an invitation to 
Sir Robert Cecil and his wife to the wedding of her son 
Thomas Posthumous and Mistress Margaret Sidney on 
Monday, 9th August, at her house in the Blackfriars. She 
wrote that there would be no “solemnity but only a private 
meeting of good and honourable friends”, and that her 
coach would be sent to court for her two daughters, Elizabeth 
and Anne Russell, Maids of Honour to the Queen, while their 
mother would “ appoint them whom they shall bring with them, 
whereof Sir Robert Sidney and Sir William Brooke to be two 
against that time ’’.1° Posthumous invited his cousin, Anthony 
Bacon, informing him that there would be no music at his wedding, 
as his mother did not wish it, and dancing was far from his own 
‘“* Humour ”’, so that he would “‘ have all let alone, and seek only 
to please the Beholders with a Sermon and a Dinner, and myself 
with beholding my Mistress’. He concluded by wishing his 
cousin’s health would allow him to be as merry as he, “Thomas, 
now had cause to be.194 After the ceremony, Lady Russell was 
at last rid of her younger son : “‘ By ye occasion of w‘h said marriage, 
the said St Thomas Posthumus Hoby hath altered and is dep’ted 
from yé place of his habitac’on and hyeth out of ye South into 
y¢ North p’tes of this Realme.” 19° Sir Thomas and Lady 
Margaret Hoby took up their residence at Hackness. 

Lady Hoby’s diary, which begins, as we now have it, just three 
years after her third marriage, excellently illustrates, albeit briefly 
and baldly, the country life of the day, its routine interrupted by occa- 
sional visits to London, to York, and to places in the surrounding 
district. “The diary itself was perhaps part only of a much larger 
book: this there seems to be no means of ascertaining. Lady Hoby 
made, in addition, other written records, for she frequently noted, 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 33 


in her diary, that she had written in her common-place book, 
and in her table-book, or had made notes, either in her sermon 
book or testament, of the sermons to which she had listened :_ but 
these, if they still exist, are not accessible. The diary is all that 
remains, and, in one important element, it is very disappointing : 
it reveals nothing whatever of Margaret’s personal relationships 
with the people about her, or the impressions made upon her by 
those she met. In this respeét the diary is barren and completely 
_ colourless; nor does the writer comment in any way upon 
contemporary events. 

Concerning her third and last husband, much may be 
gleaned from incidental allusions in contemporary correspondence. 
To Richard Cholmley, their near neighbour and owner of 
Whitby Abbey and its lands, Sir Thomas appeared as “a 
troublesome, vexatious neighbour, . . . who having married 
a widow, the inheritor of all Hackness lordship, having a full 
purse, no children (and as it was thought not able to get one), 
delighted to spend his money and time in suits. “This Sir Thomas 
exhibited four several bills in the Star Chamber against Sir Richard : 
had him also in suit in the Exchequer, where he endeavoured to 
overthrow all the Royalties and Liberties belonging to the manor of _ 
Whitby ”.1°° “These suits were occasioned largely because “ Sir 
Richard would not suffer this Hobby to carry matters upon the 
bench according to his humour, which he loved:to have satisfied, 
whether right or wrong’’.1°7 Sir Richard’s son Hugh also 
encountered his father’s old enemy, still on the Bench in 1635, 
and, though Hugh did his best to agree with him—or so he says— 
and comply with his wishes, he found Sir ‘Thomas “ of such a 
nature, unless a man became his very slave, there was not any 
keeping friendship, for he loved to carry all things after his own 
way and humour, how unjust or injurious soever”’.1°8 “There 
must have been real hatred between the families, or bitter jealousy, 
on Hoby’s part, of the popularity and power of the Cholmleys 
in the distri€t. Otherwise, it is difficult to account for some of 
Sir Thomas’s ill-natured actions. Richard Cholmley, who was 
but twenty-one at the time, though already married and the father 
of a son, was implicated in the Essex conspiracy of 1601. His 
friends endeavoured to prove that he had been “there by chance ”’, 
and was a man “of no power’; but Hoby, owing him a private 
grudge because he supported the Eures, another powerful Y orkshire 

D 


34 DPDARY VOR VIA DY SEL Chit, 


family, in their quarrel with him, hastened to write to Sir Robert 
Cecil to assure him that he, Cholmley, was able, “ within the 
liberty [of Whitby Strand], wherof his father is bailiff by 
inheritance, to raise 500 men, if they should show themselves 
as traitorous as they do already show themselves disobedient to 
Her Majesty’s laws. He is able to raise some of his confining 
neighbours where his living, named Groman Abbey (a place 
famous for priests) doth lie.” “The Cholmley lands, continued 
the good Protestant Hoby, “lieth in the most dangerous parts 
of Yorkshire for hollow hearts, for popery. “The most part 
thereof, with his chief house, lieth along the sea coast, very apt 
to entertain bad intelligenced strangers.”” Cholmley had joined 
the conspiracy, declared his loyal neighbour, because of “ his 
father’s desperate estate, who doth owe more than he can pay, 
his backwardness in religion, and to embrace civil government, 
and his alliance and love to the Earl of Essex”. In the post- 
script of this letter to Cecil, Hoby revealed his true grievance, 
injured dignity: for Henry Cholmley, Richard’s father, had, 
Hoby indignantly reported, claimed “to have the mustering of 
Her Majesty’s subjects within the liberty of Whitby Strand, and 
hath taken away my warrants which I have sent forth for Her 
Majesty’s service, I then being a commissioner for musters, and 
did send forth warrants in his own name by virtue of his bailiwick, 
when he was not commissioner ”’,19 

It is very clear that the distri€t, led by the older Catholic 
families of the Cholmleys and Eures, resented the demeanour of 
this all-important, powerfully-conne¢ted, undersized, and strictly 
conscientious Puritan new-comer : and distrust of the man, who 
was probably regarded in Yorkshire as a Government spy, was 
doubtless intensified by differences in religion. ‘Though Matthew 
Hutton, the Archbishop of York, considered Hoby to be, in 1597, 
‘“a gentleman of very great hope,’”’!° he was hated by recusants in 
Yorkshire for his uncompromising Puritanism. ‘The recorded 
opinion, therefore, of Sir William Brereton, rather than the 
biassed judgment of the Cholmleys, concerning Sir “Thomas 
is the more to be trusted. In Sir William’s eyes, Sir Thomas was 
“the most understanding, able, and industrious justice of peace 
in this kingdome. Noe warrant graunted out butt he takes notice 
thereof in a booke: and att sessions an account demaunded of all 
those warrants sent out : which, if the constables to whom they are 


DIARY ORF LADY HOBY 35 


delivered, doe not exequute nor returne, and give an account: 
they are called upon at the sessions: or if those that require and 
procure the warrants keepe them in their hands, and make use of 
them for their owne ends and doe nott deliver them to bee served, 
they are bound over to the sessions.” 111. “The constant recurrence 
of his name amongst the J.P.s of the North Riding in the records 
of the Court of Quarter Sessions bears out Brereton’s testimony 
to his energy and hard work in the local administration of justice. 
He was one of the bulwarks of the Government in this difficult 
region of the North Riding. When,in April, 1599, an attack 
was to be made on a house near Whitby, five miles from the sea, 
“ strong, large, and many conveyances underground to a brook 
running near,” a place suspected of harbouring Papists, the man 
in charge of the raid for the Government declared to Cecil that, 
now Sir ‘Thomas Hoby was in London, he knew of no faithful 
assistance in the country, while the people had even threatened 
revenge against Hoby for his zeal in hunting down the recusants,1!2 
Hackness must have been a Protestant oasis in a Papist desert. 
Rather more than a year after his marriage, in Odtober, 1597, 
Sir Thomas Hoby and Mr. Stanhope contested with Sir John Savile 
and Sir William Fairfax the parliamentary election of Knights 
of the Shire for the county of York. Upon the day, a great crowd 
assembled at York Castle, for Sir John Savile had brought in his 
train an enormous following to vote for him, and had, incidentally, 
corrupted the returning officer beforehand. So large was the 
gathering that the election, “ held in open county,”’ was decided 
by ordering the crowd to divide into two groups, whereupon 
certain gentlemen, and the returning officer, viewing them, 
decided that the group for Sir John and Sir William contained, 
“by 300 or 200 at the least,” more voters than that for Mr. 
Stanhope and Sir Thomas. ‘Therefore the former were duly 
declared elected and their indentures made, read to the people, 
and sealed accordingly. Stanhope and Hoby, however, demanded 
a recount, which, by a clever ruse, was foiled by Sir John, the 
defeated candidates alleging that many of those who had given 
their votes for Fairfax and Savile “‘ were not freeholders of forty 
shillings per annum ultra repriss”. It was characteristic of Hoby’s 
pertinacity, doubtless inherited from his mother, that the affair 
was taken before the Privy Council. In the complaint laid before 
this body, it was stated that, amongst those in the group in favour 


36 DIARY VOR ML ADDY SHO By 


of Fairfax and Savile, and helping to swell its size, were “ citizens 
and inhabitants of York, women and children and other strangers 
not having lawful voices, to the number of five or six hundred ”.443 
Hoby lost this election, but he sat, nevertheless, in the Parliament 
which met on 24th OGtober, 1597, though it was as a representative 
of the borough of Scarborough. He had already attended two 
of Elizabeth’s Parliaments : that which met 12th November, 1588, 
dissolving March, 1589, and the short session of February—April, 
1593. He then represented the borough of Appleby in West- 
moreland. His name does not appear in the list of those members 
who sat in the assembly summoned to meet 27th October, 1601, 
though Scarborough figures in the list. In March, 1604, he was 
elected again for that borough, but afterwards, till his death, 
he represented Ripon.444 By 1601 he wasalso a commissioner of 
the peace for the North and East Ridings of Yorkshire, of oyer and 
terminer, for ecclesiastical causes, for musters, and had been thrice 
a commissioner for the subsidy.1!° In that same year the Council 
of the North recommended his name for election to their body, 
and a few months later it appeared, too, in the list of High Com- 
missioners for the Province of York.1!® ‘Thus his frequent 
comings and goings, to which occasional reference is made in 
Lady Hoby’s diary, may be well understood. 

‘Throughout his life Hoby, like his mother, seems to have 
suspected his neighbours of deliberate malice towards himself, 
and to have been ever on the watch for anything approaching 
insult. In 1601 it happened that he was fined by the Justices 
of Assize for Yorkshire: immediately he protested against it 
to his cousin Cecil, declaring that it was “to his disgrace in the 
country where he dwells”’, and alleging partiality in the case 
of one of the judges, “Mr. Baron Savyll.” 147 It is certain 
that his presence in Yorkshire was resented. A contemporary 
is reported to have said of him, “that he came over the water 
wth his coach and three horses but all scante worth sixpence or 
sIx pence a peece,”’ that he was “‘ ye busyest sawcie little Jacke in 
all the Contrie, and wolld have an ore in eny bodies bote ”’, 
but that there “‘ was a sorte of yonge fellowes in Yorkeshire wolld 
plaie him a tricke ere it were longe, and when it were done he 
sholld not mende himself ”’.148 Sir Thomas was as suspicious of the 
Cholmleys as he was distrustful of his other neighbours, the Eures 
of Malton. He vowed in 1600 that there had ‘‘ been some dryness 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 37 


in the Lord Ewre (whose tenants are my next neighbours) almost 
ever since I was employed as a commissioner in these parts ;_ which, 
if it has been for my partiality, or injustice, I desire on proof 
thereof to be punished; if it be for want of partiality (as I shall 
rather prove) I hope my wrongs will appear in time which I have 
sustained’. “his feeling culminated in an ungracious squabble 
and brawl at Hackness with young William Eure, out of which 
developed a long law-suit, tried first before the Council of the 
North in York, and then in the Star Chamber in London.1!9 
Lady Hoby refers to it in her diary, and Shakespeare is conjectured 
to have had it in mind when he wrote the carousal scenes in 
Twelfth Night,!?° holding up to laughter the idiosyncracies of the 
unattractive Malvolio while depicting the distaste of the jolly 
knights for the Puritanical mode of life. Sir Thomas was still 
prickly and self-assertive in 1620 when the King himself ordered 
Secretary Naunton to write to him and instruct him to behave 
becomingly towards the President of the Council of the North.¥1 

Impartial evidence of his wife’s qualities clears Hoby of the 
charge of seeking only a fortune in his pursuit of the heiress of 
Mr. Dakins, in spite of the fact that, though he never saw her 
until after the death of her second husband, his courtship began 
when she first became a widow. Margaret was reputed to be 
“the best Lady that ever any knight in the world enioyed”. A 
young relative, Jane Lutton, who attended her for a time in her 
“religious and godly”? household, was said to have gained 
incredibly “in good things . . . which she ever acknowledgeth 
to haue reaped for spirituall profit, while she had the happyness ”’ 
to be in that family. Lady Hoby and Jane’s mother were akin 
and bore the same name, but while both were “holy and wise, 
and every way vertuous and good ”’, yet “eminency of gifts was 
in the one so much the more, as eminency of dignity and estate 
had made a difference and given an happy advantage ’’.1?2 
Francis Bacon, writing to his cousin Hoby about his own marriage, 
said ‘“‘ Your loving congratulations for my doubled life, as you call 
it, I thank you for. No man may better conceive the Joys of a good 
wife than yourself with whom I dare not compare.”’ 18 

In his will, made 28th March, 1640, Sir Thomas spoke 
affectionately of his wife, who had died in 1633.124 After a 
declaration of his religious faith, in which he stressed his belief 
in the immediate ascension of his soul into heaven, he ordered 


38 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


his body to be buried “in decent manner, but neyther with 
superfluous cost, nor in ouer publique sort’, in the chancel of 
Hackness Church, “ next vnto the dust of the body of my late most 
deare and onely wife, the Lady Margarett Hoby.” He refers to 
the “‘ extraordinary affection that was betweene her and myselfe in 
our life tyme”, and leaves to his heir, John Sydenham, the 
“ flaggon bracelett of gould, with the picture of my late most 
deare and onely wife deceased, which is fastned therevnto, and 
which I doe purpose (if God shall permitt) to weare about myne 
arme vntill and att the tyme of my death. And alsoe one other 
picture of my said late wife, that is sett in a box of ivery or 
elephantes tooth, with a peice of christall to keepe it from the dust, 
hopeing that my said executor will keepe them for memoriall of 
that affectionate care which my said late wife did, in her life tyme, 
take of his well doeing, and of his education in his youngest yeares.”’ 

Margaret, as her diary reveals, was a deeply religious woman, 
and her husband was imbued with all the intolerance of the 
fanatical Puritan. ‘There exists still a most pathetic memorial 
to her life: it is the little chapel in Harwood Dale which Hoby 
erected to her memory in 1634, soon after her death.12° Hackness 
was then, as now, a wide-flung parish, and the dwellers in the 
Dale, some six miles from their parish church, could, if they so 
wished, make climatic conditions, during much of the year, an 
excuse for absence from divine service. “This must have been a 
source of grief to Margaret, but what was probably her dearest 
wish did not assume concrete shape till after her death. “The 
chapel of St. Margaret stands in the middle of a field in a setting 
of elm trees, surrounded by lovely country of hill and dale and 
rolling moor, still lonely and sparsely populated. It would be 
difficult to find a sight more desolate than this little ruined chapel 
to-day. “The surrounding stone wall is tumbled, the roof gone 
from the lich-gate, and the tiny grave-yard is full of broken stones 
and choked with breast-high weeds. “The chapel itself is a small 
oblong rectangular building of squared stones, the stone steps of 
its doorway now broken and grass grown, most of the roof gone, 
and much of the interior a heap of rubble added to constantly 
from the crumbling walls. But the east end is solid yet,!*6 and 
bears intact upon its interior surface the slate slab inscribed to 
Margaret, though the lettering is becoming worn by the winds and 
rain which blow at will through the empty place.!®”_ It reads thus :

Margaret Lady Hoby was buried on 6th September, 1633,!% 
in the chancel of Hackness Church, seven years before the 
death of her third and last husband. He erected a monu- 
ment to her in this church also; it still is on the south 
wall of the chancel, in the form of a black marble tablet, 
surrounded by a white marble border, and bears an inscription, 
coat of arms, and various sculptured symbols. Sir Thomas’s 
tribute to his wife is interesting: “‘ The Lady Margaret Hoby, 
late wife of St Thomas Posthvmvs Hoby Knight, and sole Davghter

the seventh moneth of that yeare, it was the will of Almighty 
God to call her fovrth of this vale of miserie ; and her body was

September An° 1633) soe neer vnto the bodies of her sayde father

and endevoures to performe the whole will of God through her

‘Thomas Posthvmvs Hoby.”’ 
Margaret’s own brief record of a few years of her life bears out 
this testimony to her virtues. 

Elizabethan literature abounds in references to the practices 
of Puritan households. ‘Their religious exercises were as great a 
butt for jokes and gibes as is the mother-in-law in modern times.1°? 
Every word of her diary reveals Lady Hoby as holding, and following 
out, all the tenets of a stri€t and narrow Puritanism, and the brief 
visions of the household which are vouchsafed to us in its pages 
show that public religious exercises were daily practised therein. 
Fortunately a most interesting picture of life as it was actually 
lived in Hackness, in Lady Hoby’s day, has been preserved in 
the evidence of a servant given in the Star Chamber case which 
Sir Thomas Hoby brought in 1600 against a party of young men 
who had, on a hunting expedition, visited him and behaved 
riotously in his house. Robert Nettleton, aged twenty-three, 
told the Court that he had nine fellow men-servants at Hackness, 
William Jarden, John Wasse, Henry Brathwhaite, Peter Cample- 
man, “Thomas Wraie, and four others, while the women servants 
consisted, at this time, of Mistress Everell Aske and three widows, 
to whom Hoby, of his charity, gave shelter and board. ‘This 
witness described the coming of a hunting party of young men to 


DULARY OF LADY MOBY 4I 


Hackness. A footman was sent before them, two or three hours 
earlier, to beg the hospitality of Sir Thomas and his lady, but, when 
they finally arrived, Sir Thomas received them alone as Lady Hoby 
was sick. Immediately after their coming, declared the witness, 
they fell to cards in the great chamber, and these they must have 
brought with them, for, Nettleton declared, he knew there used 
to be none in his master’s house. At supper time, his master 
keeping them company, Mr. Stephen Hutchinson drank a health 
unto his master, and Sir William Eure did drink a health unto his 
master and to my lady’s health : to whom his master replied that, 
if drinking would make my lady well, he could find it in his heart 
to drink himself drunk but otherwise, since it would do her no good 
and himself hurt, he desired them to pardon him, for he would 
drink but his ordinary. After supper his master went first to my 
lady, and afterwards to see each of their lodgings prepared ; then 
came to them again, and, finding them still at play, told them that, 
if they would repair to their chambers, they were fit, and he would 
bring them to their lodging. “They, however, desired him to bear 
with them, for they would play awhile. Then he told them that 
he would bid them goodnight, for he would go keep my lady 
company as she was sick. Sir Thomas then gave order to his 
servants to go to prayers in the hall, which were usually, declared 
Nettleton, said in the great chamber morning and evening. His 
master also gave commandment to his chamberlain to show 
every gentleman’s man his master’s lodging, which he did. “hen, 
continued this witness, when they were singing of the psalms, 
three of the guests’ servants came and stood in the hall, laughing 
and making of.a noise during the whole time of the prayers. In 
the chamber over them the guests also made a noise, and some, 
coming out to the stairs that led down into the hall, made a noise 
with singing of strange tunes which, he was sure, were not psalms. 
The next morning breakfast was prepared, and he and others 
were in attendance. Some drank healths one to another, beer and 
wine in plenty being provided for them, and Mr. Richard Cholmley 
filled his glass so full as it ran over into the rushes, one of his 
company telling him it was a sin to spill drink. Mr. William 
Hilliard, somewhat before breakfast was done, rose from the 
table and swore he would drink no more healths. After breakfast 
the guests made a great noise in the great chamber with hallowing 
and shouting, and, my lady’s chamber being very near, Sir ‘Thomas 


42 DIARY -OPVLCADYVEOBY 


sent them word that, if they would use some other quieter exercise 
they should be welcome, for that they did disease my Lady. There- 
upon it was answered they would but see my lady and go their 
ways. Soon after Sir William Eure’s man came to Nettleton and 
required him to tell my lady that Mr. William Eure was desirous 
to see her before he went, and that then they would be gone. 
Whereupon Nettleton brought my lady’s answer which was, 
that she was not ready, but as soon as she were she would send 
him word. After, she sent for Nettleton and willed him to tell 
Mr. Eure that she would be willing to see him but not the rest of 
the company, because she was sickly and kept her chamber. To 
this Mr. Eure answered, By god thy master sends me such 
scurvy messages as I care not for them. I came not for his meat 
and his drink but to see my lady and therefore let him send me 
word what it lies him in and I will pay for it, and will set up 
horns at his gate and be gone. Whereupon Nettleton asked his 
fellow servant Jarden whether he were best to deliver the message 
in those terms, or make it better if he could. Jarden told him 
he were best to deliver it as it was for truth would never shame 
itself, which he thereupon did in my lady’s chamber and in her 
presence. Whereto Sir Thomas willed him to answer Mr. Eure 
that he wished him and his company to depart his house and ground 
quietly, and, when he was off his ground, if he sent him either that 
message or any other, he would take it. After this being delivered 
to Mr. Eure, he being in a little chamber betwixt the dining chamber 
and my lady’s, he said he came to see my lady and would see her 
ere he went, for they were strong enough to keep that chamber 
if there came twenty or forty against them. And Sir William Eure, 
looking out of a window, seeing country-men come towards the 
house, said the country was raised. Whereunto Jarden answered 
him that they were country people that came to the town about a 
commission that was appointed to be sitten on that day. Sir William 
replied that he and his company were strong enough to keep that 
little chamber against all the country, and Mr. William Eure 
said he cared not for the commission; he would tear it. After 
this my lady was willing to see Mr. William Eure, who wished 
the rest of the guests to go forth of that room into the dining 
chamber, and Jarden ordered Nettleton to bolt the door after them 
because my lady would speak only with Mr. Eure. When Nettleton 
tried to do this, the guests thrust the door open upon him, and took 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 43 


hold of him and threw him against the table end in the great 
chamber, being about two fathoms in length. And so they went 
into that little chamber again, and would not afterward suffer 
him to bolt the door. Whereupon he, Nettleton, going down into 
the hall, saw all the defendants come through the hall, and there 
George Smith, one of them, seeing a pair of stag’s horns nailed on 
the screen, swore, by god’s heart, I would the stag’s horns were 
as hard-nailed or as hard fastened upon Sir Thomas his head as 
they were there. And thereupon the said George Smith went 
forth and fetched his horse, and, having pulled up two stiles, passed 
through a newly levelled courtyard, and trampled it across to and 
fro, galloping up and down. Nettleton told him it was not well 
done, for the ground cost money before it came to that pass. Smith 
answered that he did it in despite. Nettleton also testified to 
seeing four “quarries” of glass broken in one window in his 
master’s dining chamber, which was said to be done with 
throwing of stones by one of the defendants or some of their

Such was one of the episodes in Lady Hoby’s life, and the event 
which provided Sir Thomas Hoby with an excuse for haling 
several of the young gentlemen of the neighbourhood before the 
Council of the North and finally before the Court of Star Chamber 
in London. The evidence given in this case,!8* and fortunately 
preserved, has thus provided us incidentally’ with an excellent 
picture of the manners of the period, and, in particular, of the 
attitude of the North Riding of Yorkshire towards Sir Thomas 
Hoby. 

cae Hoby’s manor of Hackness, the reétory, and her 
surrounding lands in Harwood Dale, Silpho, Suffield, Hingles, 
and Everley, were worth £1,500 a year at her death. Probably 
Hoby spent much money on his wife’s property. Perhaps because 
of this, and as they had no children, he may have felt justified, 
after much importunity since she was “ not willing ”’, in persuading 
her to settle, while she was yet alive, the manor and rectory of 
Hackness, and her other lands, on herself and her husband and 
their heirs, with remainder over to his own heirs. “The deed of 
settlement was finally completed in July, 1632. But Margaret 
had expressed a wish, and only on this condition did she agree to 
the deed, that certain of her property should be sold to raise a 
sum of money to be distributed amongst her relatives, and in 


44 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


deeds of charity, at the discretion of her husband. . This Sir 
Thomas faithfully promised, and, within a month of her death, 
in September, 1633, in order to carry out her wishes, he conveyed 
certain lands to Margaret’s kinsman, John Chapman, whom she had 
brought up, and his heirs, in trust to raise the sum of £2,700 to 
be divided as she had appointed. But no sooner was the 
property settled upon Chapman, than he proved “ wasteful ”’, 
and refused “* to be advised by the said Sir Thomas”. ‘The latter, 
accordingly, brought actions against him in the Courts of Wards 
and of Chancery in order to recover the lands, alleging that they 
were only settled on Chapman and his heirs in trust for raising 
the sum of money to be paid to Margaret’s kindred, and asserting 
that Chapman, in his debauched state, would never produce the 
money. Hoby won his suit in 1639, recovered most of the 
property which had been settled upon Chapman, and then made 
his own personal estate responsible for the legacies to his wife’s 
relatives. A part only of these did he pay before his death, as his 
will, which left the whole estate of Hackness and the lands near 
by to his cousin, John Sydenham, testified. “The young man whom 
Sir ‘Thomas made his heir was the eldest son and heir of John 
Sydenham, late of Brimpton, County Somerset, and of Hoby’s 
kinswoman, Alice Hoby, of Hales, Gloucestershire. John was, 
apparently, brought up in the Hackness household. 

Sir Thomas so framed his will that his wealthy wife’s last 
wishes, which he had promised faithfully to fulfil, assumed the 
appearance of actions proceeding from his own charity and good 
feeling. No wonder Margaret was so reluctant to settle her 
inheritance upon him. “‘ And whereas I, being desirous to be 
beneficiall ynto many of my late wife’s poore kindred and freindes, 
haue heretofore voluntarily conveyed and setled certain landes, 
parcell of my mannour of Hacknes, vpon one John Chapman 
and his heires, with intent and vpon trust that I might at my owne 
will and pleasure cause seuerall summes of money to be distributed, 
as a free gift by me giuen vnto them, as a remembrance of that 
extraordinary affection that was betweene her and myselfe in our 
life tyme. “The most part of which money I have already paid, 
as will evidently appeare by the acquittances, by me received at 
their seuerall paymentes thereof.” “ The residue of the moneyes, 
as yet vnpaid ynto my said late wives kindred,” Sir “Thomas 
intended, so the will states, to set out with his own hand in an

accompanying schedule, and this, though not complete with the 
names and sums of money at the time the will was made, was 
nevertheless signed and sealed by him in the presence of witnesses

soon after, however, Hoby was seized with a fit and died, without 
having completed the schedule by which his wife’s relations were

they found written there the name of only one of Lady Hoby’s 
relations, Miss Hester Dakins, to whom £30 had been left.1%8 
Perhaps the attack of cold palsy from which Hoby died did really 
take him by surprise: perhaps he had already paid out most of 
the money promised. ‘The fact remains, however, that it was his 
relatives, to the almost entire exclusion of his wife’s, who inherited 
her lands. ‘To this day, curiously enough, bad odour clings to his 
name in the village, and the legend is still told of how his wife’s 
end was hastened by his kicking her downstairs, whence the stains 
made by her blood could never be erased.184_ It may be also that 
he protests his affection for her over-much and too publicly to 
be truly sincere. 

Sir Thomas Posthumous Hoby died on 30th December, 1640, 
at the age of 74.185 Not until 1682 was a memorial erected to 
him on the north wall of the church of Hackness, and then by a 
man who had never known him, Sir John Posthumous Sydenham, 
son of Sir “Thomas’s heir. 

The personality of Lady Margaret Hoby is best pictured from 
the revelations of her own diary in its entirety: therefore it has 
been transcribed im extenso, for extraéts, even when well-chosen, 
spoil the whole and inevitably suffer when taken out of their 
context. 

The diary is one of the best illustrative sources extant for 
the life and work of the women of the land-owning families 
at the end of the sixteenth century.186 

Covering only a few years, 1599-1605, it was written at a time 
of great depression in England. Increased taxation had resulted 
from the wars with Spain, and this in turn reacted inevitably upon 
trade. “There were bad harvests from 1594-98, and again in 
1600: corn had risen to 80s. a quarter in 1596, and later reached 
120s. “Lhe year 1603 was, in addition, one of the worst plague 
years England had ever experienced, and in 1604, although 


46 DIARY OF CADDY HOBBY 


London was comparatively healthy, the sickness was still rife in 
the country distri¢ts. “Trade revived somewhat after the accession 
of James I, but only momentarily, for fear of infe¢tion brought 
business almost to a standstill, and English goods were disliked 
abroad because of it.18?_ "The political events occurring at this time 
are too well known to require comment: the Essex conspiracy, 
the death of Elizabeth, and the peaceful coming of James of 
Scotland. 

The most interesting points about Lady Hoby are unfortunately 
those which are not obvious nor easily ascertained from the diary. 
We should like to know far more of that education which could 
produce a woman who, at the age of twenty-eight, spent a large 
proportion of each day in religious exercises, in study and serious 
reading, and yet who never neglected her household and estate, 
who had time to keep accounts and supervise those kept by her 
servants, to write her diary and other notes, to practise surgery and 
physic at home and in the village, and to educate her servants 
and the neighbourhood in religious principles. Her relaxations 
were few: bowling, fishing, boating, and playing the alpherion, 
for when she was not reading her fingers were ever busy at her 
“work”. She puts one in mind of the second wife of “Thomas, 
Lord Berkeley, a lady who, “living at Yate, Callowdon, and 
other her Country houses, would betimes in Winter and Somer 
mornings make her walkes to visit her stable, barnes, day house, 
pultry, swinetroughs, and the like.’’18§ 

Judging from the authors to whose books Lady Hoby had access, 
the dwellers in Hackness did not indulge in one, at least, of the 
vaunted pleasures of rural society, that of listening to “ pleasant 
mad-headed knaves that be properly learned, and will read in 
divers pleasant books and good authors, such as Guy of Warwick, 
The Four Sons of Aymon, The Ship of Fools, The Budget of Demands, 
The Hundred Merry Tales, The Book of Riddles, and many other 
excellent writers—both witty and pleasant.” 48° “The volumes 
with the perusal of which Lady Hoby filled her leisure moments 
may have been partly responsible for her apparent conviction 
of her own utter sinfulness. Particularly when she was afflicted 
with a headache or the toothache did this certainty of guilt, 
and its consequent direct Divine punishment, increase and deepen. 
Of the influences which moulded her in infancy and adolescence, 
forces which could also, by their workings, have produced in her 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 47 


this same belief, we know scarcely anything. Her father’s 
personality is a mystery. Amongst the books of Ripon Minster 
is a little volume in thirteenth century writing, a summary of the 
Bible in Latin verse, which has eight pages of prose at the beginning, 
consisting of meditations De Magnitudine, De Pulcritudine, etc. 
In the margin of one page of this metrical paraphrase is written, 
in a later hand, “ Arthure Dakyns.”14° ‘This may have been her 
father’s : again, it may have been her cousin’s. At some period 
in her youth, we know that Margaret was in the service of the 
puritanical Earl and Countess of Huntingdon.444 Again, the 
references in her diary to her mother, a capable woman seemingly 
busied with the care of her own separate estates, are frequent, 
but not illuminating. Mistress Thomizine Dakins was buried 
in Hackness Church on 13th November, 1613. Which of these 
was most responsible for making Margaret what she became? 
Who were the teachers from whom she acquired her ability to 
read and write, to set down her thoughts upon paper, to act as a 
competent housewife, estate bailiff, village doctor, and mistress 
to the young girls of good family brought by their parents to serve 
in her household? Upon what principles was she educated, that 
she became imbued with her love of sermons and theology, and 
developed a taste for heavy religious literature which, with her 
Bible, comprised almost all her range of reading? 142 Daily she 
seems to have studied the Bible, but of a knowledge of languages, 
similar to Lady Russell’s, there is no trace. Lady Hoby has, 
however, left no account of herself, so far as is known, except her 
diary, which was begun after her third marriage. 

‘This daily record is of great value as a contemporary document, 
though it is not nearly so full as we would have it. ‘The religious 
element is prominent, to the exclusion of much else, for the 
record seems to have been kept largely as a means of assisting 
in the religious exercise of self-examination, and only partly because 
It was useful in other ways. Lady Hoby is the “ earliest known 
British woman diarist 143 Her daily personal record is very 
introspective, yet she shows no real capacity for self-knowledge 
or ability in self-analysis, for she sets down more or less conventional 
religious expressions of self-disparagement. A perusal of the 
contents of the diary gives one to think that she may have written 
with an eye on a possible reader, for we are rarely allowed aglimpse 
of the living woman. In spite of this barrier which she herself 


48 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


has raised, every detail of the record increases our acquaintance 
with her and our sympathy for her. She is interesting because 
she matters so much to herself. 

Lady Hoby emulated, in one respect at least, the example 
of her old mistress, the Countess of Huntingdon, for she 
received into her childless household at Hackness many girls of 
good family. “There is constant reference to them in her diary, 
but, except for a few sentences, she tells us little of the training 
they received there. She was herself a good and religious woman ; 
prayers and meditations frequently occupied her, though not to the 
exclusion of more mundane matters. It was necessary that she 
should organize her resources that there might always be adequate 
provision of food, clothing, and light for the large household, 
and she is revealed in her diary as a good business woman. Her 
husband was much away engaged in Parliamentary duties or in 
the administration of local justice, and Lady Hoby supervised 
their large estate in his absence. She was in touch with all the 
affairs of the countryside : her house was the centre of the neigh- 
bourhood, so that the girls in her household could not fail to benefit 
from her example. She listened to the catechising of her servants 
every evening, to the “ le¢tor ”” which was read to them, or to the 
“repetition ’’ of the Sabbath Day’s sermons. She took part in 
the singing of psalms, in the prayers which were held in the great 
hall morning and evening; she read to, and talked with, her 
women concerning household matters, or “ good things ”’, or 
“some principles’ of religion. In person she supervised those 
in her service, but we search her diary in vain for details of the 
complicated sixteenth century housewifery. We must perforce 
be satisfied with such vague references as “* I wrought and instructed 
som of my famelie” ; “I saw a man’s leg dressed”; “I took 
order” for dinner or supper; “I wrought with my maids till 
almost night”; “I went about amongst them ”’, or “into the 
fields with them ”’; or “ I went abroad with my maids that were 
pulling hemp”. ‘To the actual members of the household there 
are only brief allusions in the diary, but we know, from another 
contemporary account, that, in 1600, there were ten men-servants 
in the house, if not more, together with Mistress Everell Aske, 
and three widows whom Sir Thomas boarded, of his charity, 
** giuinge them meate and drink and two pence a peece a week ”’ ; 
and also “an other man”’, to whom he likewise gave alms.144 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 49 


There are two other women diarists contemporary with Lady 
Hoby, and, from what we know of their early lives, the gaps in 
her story may perhaps be filled. Her life and work will certainly 
be the better understood after comparison with the education 
and pursuits of other women of her class. 

Perhaps Grace Sherrington’s description of her own up- 
bringing at home illustrates that of most similarly placed girls 
in the sixteenth century. She herself wrote the story of her youth 
when she was growing old, so that it is a reminiscence rather than 
a diary. Her Journal is a thick black volume, filled with neat 
writing, and is still preserved. Born about 1552, Grace was the 
second of the three daughters and co-heiresses of Sir Henry 
Sherrington of Laycock Abbey, Wiltshire. Her childhood was 
spent at Laycock, she and her sisters being brought up in their 
father’s house by stern and careful parents and a much loved 
governess, a poor relation in the household, one Mistress Hamblyn, 
her father’s niece. Grace’s mother, like Lady Jane Grey’s parent, 
used to beat her severely in order to instil the precepts of virtue, 
and “‘never so much as for lying”. She had also brought up 
the poor relation, and trained her to become a suitable governess 
for the children. “This woman, Grace wrote in later life, ‘‘ proved 
very wyse, religious and chaste, all good virtues that might be in 
a woman very constantly settled in her, for from her youth she 
made good use of all things that ever she did see or hear, and 
observed all companys that ever she came in, good or bad, so that 
she could give a right censure and true judgement of most things 
and give wyse counsell upon any occasion. And she could 
apprehend and contrive any matter whatsoever propounded 
unto her, most judiciously, and sett her mynde down in wryting, 
either by letters indited or otherwise, as well as most men could 
have done. She had also good knowledge in phisick and surgerie. 
She was of an excellent quicke spiritt and plesently conceited ; 
her mirth was very savoury and full of wit; and in her sadnesse 
she uttered forth nothing but wisdom and gravitie. She scoffed 
at all dalliance, idle talk, and wanton behaviour appertayning 
thereto, with a touch of conceit to the heed thereof. She 
counselled us when we were alone so to behave ourselves as yf 
all the worlde did looke upon us, and to doe nothing in secret 
whereof our conscience might accuse us, and by any means to 
avoyd the company of serving men, or any of lyke disposition, 

E 


50 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


whose ribald talk and ydle gestures and evill suggestions were 
dangerous for our chaste ears and eyes to hear and behold. And 
further she advised us to deal truly and faithfully in all things, 
both in small matters and in greate, to beware of all lyes and oathes 
and reporting of newes; to heare much and to speake little, 
seeming to be ignorant in some things rather than to boast of 
knowledge that we have not, and thereby give occasion to be 
laughed att.’”” Grace was so fond of her wise cousin that she 
would not willingly leave her side, and very cheerfully bore 
reproof from her. “And when she did see me ydly disposed 
she would sett me to cypher with my pen, and to cast up and prove 
_ great sums and accompts, and sometimes set me to wryte a supposed 
letter to this or that body concerning such and such things, and 
other tymes let me read in Dr. ‘Turner’s Herball and Bartholomew 
Vigoe, and other times sett me to sing psalmes, and other times 
sett me to some curious work ; for she was an excellent work- 
woman in all kinds of needlework, and most curiously she would 
perform it. She gave me warning of a gentlewoman full of words 
and questions and of an undermyning disposition, a busybody and a 
medler in matters which concerned her not, neither regarding 
what hurt or trouble might redound to many thereby, nor how 
my reputation might be brought in question by conversing with 
her, for she sought much into my companye. And surely this 
counsell which she gave me was very sweet and good to be 
observed, for in myne owne experience I have found the dangers 
very great. Alsoe there was a man and woman, both marryed 
but not man and wyfe, of impudent behaviour one towards another, 
which when she behelde she asked me yf I did not think it a 
monstrous spectacle to behold, and wished me to make one stave 
and she would make another untill there were four or five verses 
made thereupon, which she performed herselfe very wittily and 
sharp against such licentious behaviour; and this she did for 
myne instruction to take heed of the lyke and to abhor and despise 
the same. Also, she advised me to avoyd such companye by all 
means possible, and to take heed of whom I received gifts, as a 
book wherein might be some fine words whereby I might betray 
myself unawares, or gloves or apples or such like, for that wicked 
Companions would ever presente treacherous attempts; which 
afterwards I found to be true in some sort and remembered her 
counsell in all these things, and more than I can here recite, which 


DEAR) OF LAD Yih O RY 51 


she thought profitable for me to remember to be instructed in, 
whereby she sent me furnished into the world. 

‘* Alsoe my father could not abyde to see a woman light or 
unstable in her carriage, to hold her head one way, her hands 
another, and her feete a third way, her eyes tossing about in 
every place and her features disfigured by an evill countenance ; 
but he lyked a woman well graced, with a constant and settled 
countenance, and a good behaviour throughout all her parts, 
which presenteth unto all men a good hope of stablished mynde 
and a virtuous disposition in her. I have seen him with his owne 
hands (for example’s sake) scourge a young man, naked from his 
girdle upwards, with fresh rods, for making but a showe and 
countenance of a saucie and unreverent behaviour towards us his 
children, and put him from his service. 

‘““Yf all fathers and mothers were so provident and careful, 
and governors and governesses put in trust by them were so 
diligent and faithfull in performing theyre trust, so many parents 
should not be discomfited as they are in theyr age by the wicked- 
ness and misfortunes of theyr children, which is too ordinary and 
lamentable to consider.” Thus was Grace trained to become 
awife. Her parents arranged her marriage before she was fifteen, 
and at sixteen she was wedded to a gay and gallant but unwilling 
bridegroom, Anthony Mildmay, son of that stern and narrow 
Puritan, Sir Walter Mildmay, and his wife Mary, the sister of 
Sir Francis Walsingham. Sir Walter was the founder of 
Emmanuel College, Cambridge, and the friend of Henry Earl of 
Huntingdon. ‘Too quiet and well brought up to suit her husband, 
Grace was brought by him to his father’s house, where heproceeded 
to leave her and spend much of his time away in the service of the 
Court. Her very necessary education was continued for many 
years under the eye of her mother-in-law, who taught her, 
amongst other arts, “to become a faithful wyfe unto her son.” 
She seems greatly to have admired her stern father-in-law, bu 
of his wife she says very little beyond that she was virtuous and 
dutiful to her husband. Not attracted to the Court life led by 
her husband, she stayed quietly at home with her parents-in-law, 
perfecting her accomplishments, and in her Journal thus describes 
her life at this period: “* My husband was much away; and 
I spent the best part of my youth in solitarinesse, shunning all 
opportunities to remove into company, lest I might be inticed 


52 DIARY °O B) DADDY FiO py, 


and drawn away by evill suggestion. And some great personages, 
ladies of my acquaintance, would persuade me to go with them 
to the Court, to feastes, marryages, and plays, saying it was a 
pity my youth should be swallowed up without all pleasure or 
delight in the world. Myne answer was that God had placed 
me in the world in this house; and, if I found no comfort here, 
I would never seeke it out of this house ; and this was my certaine 
resolution. And God did put into my mynde many good delights, 
wherein I spent my time allmost continually. First in divinitie 
every day, as my leasure would give me leave, and the Grace of 
God permitt and drawe me. I did read a chapter in the book of 
Moses, another in one of the prophets, one chapter in the Gospells, 
another in the Epistles to the end of Revelation, and the whole 
psalmes appointed for the day. Also every day I spent some tyme 
in playing on my lute, and setting songs of five parts thereunto, 
and practised my voice in singing of psalmes and prayers and con- 
fessing my sinnes, which were ever ready to meet me in every 
thought and to turne me always from God and from goodnesse. 
Also every day I spent some time in the Herball and books of 
phisick, and in ministering to one or other by the directions of 
the best phisitions of myne acquaintance ; and ever God gave a 
blessing thereunto. Also every day I spent some tyme in works of 
myne owne invention, without sample or pattern before me for 
carpett or cushion worke, and to drawe flowers and fruitt to their 
lyfe with my plumett upon paper. All which varietie did greatly 
recreate my mynde; for I thought of nothing else but that I was 
doing in every particular one of these exercises. And, though I 
was but meanly furnished to be excellent in any of those exercises, 
yet they did me good, inasmuch as I found myselfe that God 
wrought with me in all.” 

‘Thus was Grace’s youth spent. After twenty long years, 
the death of Sir Walter gave a separate household to the couple, 
and when at last Grace became the mistress of her husband’s 
house, at Apethorpe, Northamptonshire, her wisdom and 
capabilities in the management of house and estate soon became 
well known. 

Lady Mildmay did not write the story of her life until she 
was old, and then she put into it much deep thought upon the 
education of her only child, Mary, and, in the preface to the 
Journal, of children generally. She believed in the importance, 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 53 


for the general education of the young, of sound religious teaching, 
of a knowledge of the Bible and of history, and stressed, at the 
same time, the advantage of good surroundings and the necessity 
of a careful choice of teachers and companions. Her Journal 
gives a complete picture of her life as child and wife.14° Her 
volumes of prescriptions and recommendations, classified and 
copied out by her daughter, and her household books, carefully 
written in her own hand, are also still preserved, and show how wide 
a field women had for their work in this sphere, and how much 
they did in the country in the absence of all provision for medical 
aid. Lady Mildmay’s daughter, who, in her turn, married at the 
age of seventeen, wrote that her mother “ spent a great part of her 
days in the search and practice ”’ of “‘ man’s body drugs, preparations 
of medicines, and signs of disease.” 146 

On her wedding-day Grace received from her father-in-law 
a ring with the legend “* Maneat inviolata fides’, which language 
she apparently understood, for she records the motto in her Journal 
and writes that she received it “‘ in the very instant of my marryage 

. most religiously, with a full resolution by the Grace of God 
to performe the same vnto the ende of my lyfe”. Later on, her 
father-in-law’s foundation of Emmanuel materially benefited 
from her bounty. Many a young couple spent the first few years 
of married life beneath the roof of their parents until by inheritance 
or purchase they could acquire a separate establishment, and the 
inexperienced wives doubtless owed much of their training in 
housewifery and account-keeping to the older generation. 
Certainly when Grace became mistress of a large household, 
after twenty years in the strict Puritan atmosphere of the Mildmay 
family, she put into practice what she had learned there. Her 
Journal is full of her simple religious creed, and her knowledge and 
love of the Bible. She invented and worked out a system of poor 
relief on her husband’s estate, and, like Lady Hoby, minutely 
supervised every detail of house-keeping and service. When 
James I was entertained at Apethorpe in 1603, it was recorded 
that “ the tables were newly covered with costly banquets wherein 
everything that was most delicious for taste proved more delicate 
by the Arte that made it seeme beauteous to the eye: the Ladye 
of the house being one of the most excellent confectioners in 
England, though I confesse many honourable women very 
expert, ’? 447 


54 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


Lady Mildmay’s portrait was painted in 1613, when she was 
sixty-two, and shows her holding a small clasped volume, apparently 
a prayer book, while on a table behind her lie another book, open, 
and two retorts or stills. “This book is Lady Mildmay’s Book of 
Simples. One of her volumes, still in the possession of her 
descendants, containing her name and some rhyming lines, is 
De Beza’s Pithie summe of the Christan Faith. On the back of 
her picture was written what is supposed to have been her favourite 
motto, ‘‘ The minde continualli imployed in good thinges avoideth 
evill, pleaseth God, and promiseth an happie end.” 148 Some 
specimens of the elaborate needlework, which she and her daughter 
used to do, also remain. Her epitaph in Apethorpe Church, 
on the monument erected by her daughter, describes her as “ most 
devout, unspotedly chaste Mayd, Wife, and Widow, compassionate 
in Heart, and charitably helpful with Physick, Cloathes, Nourish- 
ment, or Counsel to any in Misery. She was most careful and 
wise in managing worldly estate so as her life was a blessing to 
hers, and in her death she blessed them, which happened July 27th, 
LOGO Ine 

The other woman diarist of the period is the Lady Anne 
Clifford, cousin by marriage to Lady Margaret Hoby, whose 
third mother-in-law, it will be recolleéted, married John Russell, 
the maternal uncle Anne never knew. Anne’s mother was 
Margaret Russell, the daughter of Francis, second Earl of Bedford, 
and her father was George, Earl of Cumberland. Anne, born in 
1590, was married first, in 1609, to RichardSackville, who became 
soon afterwards Earl of Dorset. She developed into a truly 
remarkable woman. After becoming a widow for the second 
time she evinced a passion for bricks and mortar equalled only 
by that of Bess of Hardwick at an earlier date. She supervised 
the management of her great estates in the North, living at each 
of her half-dozen castles in turn, and early showed a persistence 
and masterfulness like that of which her beloved mother had 
revealed herself capable in the law-suits they fought together to 
regain the Cumberland inheritance from the male side of the 
family. Children, grandchildren, and tenants alike, Anne ruled 
with a rod of iron.15° 

Unfortunately no portion of her own original day-to-day 
diary remains: Anne Clifford is best known from the mighty 
records of herself and her family which she caused to be compiled 


DIARYVOR? CA DY PHO-BY 55 


under her own supervision towards the end of her life. The source of 
these may have been diaries or accounts originally written by herself, 
but of which no trace remains. ‘They certainly embody a mass 
of detail impossible to memorize. Of her own early compositions, 
there exists to-day only an eighteenth century transcript of a 
narrative which covers but a few years. ‘This begins in the form 
of a reminiscence dealing with the year 1603, and then jumps, 
without explanation, to 1616. “Thence it continues, as a day-to- 
day diary, through 1617 to 1619, omitting completely any descrip- 
tion of the events of 1618.15! 

We cannot therefore compare, except as regards their subject 
matter, the diaries of Margaret Hoby and Anne Clifford. The 
contrast in the kind of literature read by these two women is 
interesting. Lady Anne’s social position and wealth, as wife 
of the Marquis of Dorset, were far superior to Lady Hoby’s ; 
the former was in closer contaét with the Court circle, and thus her 
reading, at this period of her life, was lighter and more fashionable 
than that of her older contemporary. Anne’s diary mentions, 
among other volumes with which she was acquainted, a History 
of the Netherlands, Montaigne’s Essays, The Faerie Queen, a book 
on the government of the ‘Turks, Chaucer, Ovid’s Metamorphoses, 
and St. Augustine’s City of God. Poetry, history, religious works, 
all were read and enjoyed, in addition to the serious and frequent 
Study of the Bible. | 

As a married lady Anne’s pursuits were very different from the 
chaste Puritan occupations of Margaret, mainly absorbed in 
church-goings, and visits to York and London in pursuit of law 
or physic. Anne played at Barley Break upon the bowling green, 
witnessed the performance of masques and plays at Court, sang, 
played upon the viol, and at Tables and Glecko, and made cushions 
of Irish stitch work. Nevertheless, serious work was not neglected. 
She records, one day, that she dressed a chamber in the afternoon, 
and set up the green velvet bed on which she and her lord were to 
lie that night.4°* Another day she spent with Marsh, “ who did 
write the chronicles of 1607,” 15% perhaps from his mistress’s 
own notes. On yet another she signed with her own hand thirty- 
three letters to her tenants in Westmorland.154 Accounts also 
Anne kept herself, for a few odd pages from one of her last account- 
books have survived. 

The question of the method of Anne’s up-bringing and education 


56 DIARY OFLA DY) ti OB, 


naturally presents itself. Her father and mother were nineteen 
and sixteen, respectively, when they married, and had _ probably 
been brought up together during part of their youth. George 
Clifford, at the age of eleven, became the Earl of Bedford’s ward 
upon the death of his father, and was in the Earl’s household until 
he was sent to Oxford. One of his tutors there was Dr. John 
Whitgift, who afterwards became Archbishop of Canterbury.1°° 
‘* Although he never attained to any great perfection in the Latin 
tongue, yet he had a general knowledge and an insight into all the 
Arts, and especially into the Mathematicks, wherein he took 
great delight and was so exquisitely versed in the same that it was 
thought to be one principal Cause of his applying himself to sea 
voyages and to Navigations, especially towards the West Indies, 
and those New found Lands, wherein he became the most knowing 
and eminent man, of a Lord, in his time.” 15° ‘The greater part 
of the Earl of Cumberland’s married life—and much of his patri- 
mony—was occupied in building ships and fitting them out for 
privateering expeditions and voyages of adventure, of which he 
made at least twelve, and from which enterprises eventually sprang - 
the East India Company. His last few years were spent on land 
but apart from his wife, owing to an estrangement between 
them over his extravagance in money matters, and a woman or 
women.15? 

The Earl of Bedford’s house, in which Anne’s father and mother 
were educated, was said to have been a “ very school of vertue ”, 
for the Earl was a great patron of letters. He was also a zealous 
supporter of the Reformation, and in prison for a time in Mary’s 
reign for his religious opinions, afterwards living for a while in 
Geneva.4°8 Margaret, his third daughter and seventh child, 
was, until after the age of seven, brought up by a maternal aunt 
at Lilford, Northamptonshire, for her own mother had died 
soon after she was born. At the end of that period she returned 
to live with her father and his second wife in one of their many 
houses, Chenies in Buckinghamshire, Woburn in Bedfordshire, 
Exeter in Devon. How she was educated, or what she learned, 
is not definitely known, but both she and her sister Anne, who 
became Countess of Warwick, were accomplished women. 

Margaret grew up into a woman of great acumen and deter- 
mination. From her husband’s death in 1605 to her own in 


1616,1°9 she fought by law to win back, for her only daughter, 


DEY Or LAY 2 FLO BY, 57 


the inheritance left by the will of her husband to a male heir in 
the person of his brother, and she caused to be prepared for this 
purpose great books of reference into which were copied all the 
family deeds.16° 

In literature her interests would seem to have been pre- 
dominantly religious. In 1591 Henry Peacham dedicated to her 
and to her sister, Anne, Countess of Warwick, 4 Sermon on the 
last three verses of the fir chapter of Fob, and William Perkins 
addressed to her one of his religious treatises. She employed 
‘Thomas ‘T'ymme, himself the author of a popular book of devotion, 
and a clever translator, to render into English, for her own edifica- 
tion, the Latin of Dudley Fenner’s Sacred Divinitie or the Truth 
which 1s according to Pietie, Described after the lawes of the 
onely and true methode and digested into ten bookes1® In 
her will she bequeathed two books, a Bible to Mr. Shute, 
“preacher,” and “* Duplesses booke of the Sacrament of the Masse ”’ 
to her niece, Lady Herbert,1®* who was Lady Hoby’s sister-in-law. 

Her letters reveal that the Countess of Cumberland possessed 
well-trained business instinéts and an ability to administer her 
husband’s estates while he was from home.1®* She was a party 
with others in obtaining a patent from the Crown for obtaining 
sea-coal and using it in the smelting of iron. Many of her epistles 
the Countess herself wrote in “a strange, cramped and irregular 
hand-writing ”’, while she was no exception to the period in that 
she “ spelled as she liked ”’, in various phonetic or partially phonetic 
forms, and thought nothing of varying the order of the letters 
in the spelling of the same word three or four times in one 
communication. In 1603 she wrote to the Countess of Shrews- 
bury, ““ Madam I hope your ladyship will excuse that I answer 
not your letter with my own hand, but your ladyship knows how 
bad a Secretary I am which in these many lines would so much 
trouble you.” 164 

Of her mother, Anne recorded that she “had a great sharp 
Natural Wit, so as there was few worthy knowledge, but she had 
some insight into them, for tho she had no language but her own, 
yet was there few books of worth translated into English but she 
read them, whereby that excellent Mind of hers was much 
enriched . . . She was a lover of the Study and prattice of 
Alchimy, by which she found out excellent Medicines, that did 
much good to many; she delighted in Distilling of waters, and 


58 DIARY GORGE AD YEO Ga 


other Chymical extractions, for she had some knowlege in most 
kind of Minerals, herbs, flowers and plants.” She was, wrote Anne, 
“truly Religious, Devout and Conscientious, even from her very 
childhood, and did spend much time in reading the Scriptures 
and other good books, and in heavenly Meditations and in prayers 
fastings and Deeds of Charity.” ‘The early death of her two sons 
did so much afflict her as that ever after “ the booke of Jobe was her 
dayly companion ”’.165 

Such was Anne’s background. Her own education inevitably 
reflected it, since she was the only surviving child of her parents 
and much attached to both. How large a part Tudor ladies played 
in the practical education of their sons and daughters itis impossible, 
from the evidence available, to state with certainty: countless 
allusions in the correspondence of the period, however, evince their 
deep interest in the process. “The Earl of Cumberland’s last 
letter to his wife before his death was a reminder of that duty in 
which she did not fail: ‘‘ Before the presence of God, I command 
thee, and in the nearest love of my heart I desire thee, to take great 
care that sweet Nan, whom God bless, may be carefully brought 
up in the fear of God, not to delight in worldly vanities, which 
I too well know be but baits to draw her out of the heavenly 
kingdom.” 16° In her will, Anne says she was “ borne, bred, 
and educated ”’ by her “ blessed mother ” in the “ true orthodox 
faith and religion established and maintained”? by the Church 
of England. “ Bred up” for some time in her infancy by her 
great-aunt Mistress Alice Elmers of Lilford, Northamptonshire, 
she was there “seasoned with the grounds of goodness and 
Religion ”’,16? but her childhood was spent mostly with her 
mother, who did, “‘ with Singular ease and tenderness of affection, 
educate ”’ and bring her up, seasoning her youth with the grounds 
of true Religion and Moral Virtue, and all other qualities befitting 
heroirth 75" 

Before Anne was twelve, she was learning to dance and was 
studying music, and, earlier than this, had been taught to read and 
write. For a brief period Samuel Daniel, the poet, was her tutor, 
but it is doubtful if she was at that time sufficiently precocious 
to derive from him that taste for poetry, history, and the classics 
which she later showed herself to possess. He exhorted her after- 
ward, in one of his poems, to store “ that better part, the mansion 
of your mind ” with 


DIARY OF LADY HOBY 59 


* the richest furniture of worth 
To make ye highly good as highly born, 


And set your virtues equal to your kind.” 


In 1599 he dedicated his poem, 4 General Defence of 
Learning, to her mother.1® But Anne, like Grace Sherrington, 
and perhaps like Margaret Hoby, had a governess, one Mistress 
Anne ‘Taylour, portrayed beside Daniel in Anne’s Great Picture 
at Appleby Castle, and described beneath as ‘‘ Governesse to this 
Young Lady, a Religious and good Woman”. She was the 
daughter of one Mr. Cholmley, born in London, and the mother 
of many children who all died before her.1”° 

By her father’s wishes, Anne was not, we learn from her own 
words, “admitted to learn any language,” but “for all other 
knowledge fit for her sex, none was bred up to greater perfection 
than herself.” 171 In the Great Picture at Appleby she is certainly 
surrounded by books, and her will, made in 1676, mentioned 
specifically, as a valuable part of the property she left, her 
“books”? in her castles of Appleby, Brougham, Brough, and 
Pendragon, and in her castle of Skipton, and tower of Barden. 
Moreover, it was she who, in 1620, erected the first monument 
to the poer Spenser in Westminster Abbey.1”2 

Possessed apparently of a knowledge of law and politics, and 
fully capable of dealing with all the problems of estate management, 
with an indomitable spirit which bowed neither to King nor 
High Court Judges, Lady Anne became a great power in the 
north-west of England. In neither of her marriages was she 
ideally matched, and this seems to have guided her, when every-day 
duties had been performed, to find solace in literature and religion, 
and the compiling of family records. “ I must confess . . . that 
tho . . . I was born a happy Creature in Mind, body, and fortune, 
and that those two lords of mine, to whom I was afterwards by 
the Divine providence Marryed, were in their Several kinds 
worthy Noblemen as any then were in this Kingdom, yet was it 
my Misfortune to have contradi€tions and Crosses with them 
both . . . so as in both their life times, the Marble Pillars of 
Knowle in Kent, and Wilton in Wiltshire, were to me oftentimes 
but the gay arbour of anguish, insomuch .. . that. . . I gave 
myself wholly to retiredness as much as I could, in both these 
great families, and made good books and virtuous thoughts my 
Companions.” 173 Certainly the memorials of her family which 


60 DIARY Of \CADY, HOBBY 


Anne, late in life, caused to be compiled, have an apt Biblical 
quotation at the end of every paragraph, which shows an astounding 
familiarity with the Scriptures either on her part or on that of 
her amanuensis. 

A vivid contemporary picture of her has been drawn for us 
in the sermon which the Bishop of Carlisle preached at her 
funeral in 1676. He commended her particularly for her care in 
the religious instruction of her household. ‘That all might be fit 
and well prepared to come to the Lord’s Supper, on the great 
festivals of the Church, “‘she took care that several books of 
Devotion and Piety might be provided four times in the year, 
that every one might take their choice of such book as they had 
not before, by which means those that had lived in her house long, 
and she seldom turned any away, might be furnished with books 
of Religion and Devotion in every kind.” She herself never 
omitted her daily reading of the Psalms for the day, and the 
hearing of a large portion of the Scriptures. Evidence of her 
knowledge of the Bible has already been noted: the good Bishop 
declared, “It was her companion.” He remarked also upon 
her foresight and prudence, and noted the fact that “she kept 
exact accounts weekly in books of her own Method, and the totals 
were duly signed with her own hand”. Moreover, continued 
the Bishop, “ she had great Sharpness of Witt, a faithfull Memory 
and deep Judgement, so that by the help of these, much reading and 
Conversation with persons eminent for learning, she had early 
gained a knowledge, as of the best things, so an ability to Discourse 
in all commendable Arts and Sciences, as well as in those things 
which belong to persons of her birth and sex to know! She would 
Discourse with Virtuosos, Travellers, Scholars, Merchants, 
Divines, States Men, and with good House-Wives in any kind, 
Insomuch that a Prime and Elegant Witt, Dr. Donne. . . is 
reported to have said of this Lady, in her younger years to this 
effect, that she knew well how to Discourse of all things, from 
Predestination to Slea Silk !” 

All things pertaining to the spindle she understood, declared the 
Bishop—wool and flax, fine linen and silk : at the same time she 
had wisdom and knowledge of those things which make for 
salvation, and sought for and followed the teaching of Christ. 
“ Authors of Several kinds of learning, Some of Controversies 
very obstruse, were not unknown unto her . . . She was not 


DRAIN Xe AD eA Yk OS 61 


ignorant of knowledge in any kind, which might make her 
Conversation not only usefull and grave, but also pleasant and 
delightfull which, that she might better do, she would frequently 
bring out of the rich Storehouse of her Memory, things New and 
Old, Sentences or Sayings of remark, which she had read, or learned 
out of Authors, and with these her Walls, her bed, her hangings, 
and furniture must be adorned, causing her Servants to write 
them in papers, and her Maids to pin them up that she, or they, 
in the time of their dressing, or as occasion served, might remember 
and make their descants on them, so that though she had not many 
books in her Chamber, yet it was dressed up with the flowers of a 
Library.””4 Ofher diary writing, the Bishop said that the Countess 
wrote down, dictated, or caused to be written, what she considered 
most important in her life, so that she might pass it in review 
before her, thinking she was better so employed, than in tattling 
or paying visits, and that some of these diaries were later put aside 
as worthless, while others were turned into annals. 

Such are three of the women who kept diaries in the last half 
of the sixteenth, and the first half of the seventeenth, century. 
So far as we know, Lady Hoby’s is the earliest original record 
now extant. “hese three chronicles reveal many points common 
to their writers ; some, it may be, typical of the period, and of 
all the educated women of the time. Each studied the Bible, but 
read also other books : each was brought up in a strict and religious 
atmosphere : each was a good housewife, with much responsibility, 
and yet managed to cultivate literary tastes and studious habits. It 
may be noted, also, that the characteristics and abilities of these 
writers must have developed and been fostered under the guidance 
of the women folk of the households in which they received 
their training. Further, all three ladies lived, for the greater 
part of their lives, in country districts in which their houses 
were the important centres of every aspect of life, economic, 
intellectual, physical and religious.

Death of Doctor Brewer, Procured by a medeson he minestred 
to him selfe to Cause him to sleep: I was much greued for it, 
because of the familiaritie I had with him, and good I had receiued 
from, but, after better aduice, I found the mercie and power of 
god shewed in openinge his eies touchinge me, and shuttinge 
them against him selfe, by Causinge him to haue great Care of 
ministringe vnto me, and so litle for his owne saftie : therefore 
I may truly conclude it is the Lord, and not the phisision, who both 
ordaines the medesine for our health and orderethe the ministring 
of it for the good of his children, closinge and vnclosinge the 
Iudgmentes of men at his pleasure: therefore let euerie one 
phisision and pattente Call vpon the Lord for a blessinge : and 
then, after Lector, I wen to bed:

[Odober Saterday the 13]

[Munday the 15 day] 

After priuat prairs I did take my leave of Mr Hoby and, sonne after,

praied, and so went to supper: after, I hard a good le¢tor and, 
after that, talked with Mr Wilsone, a godly Preacher, and so 
went to bed 

[Thursday 18] 

After I had praied with Mr Rhodes, I did eat my breakfast and

I Came hom to dimer, neccltinge my Costomarie manner of

after dinner we talked a whill and then went to church, whor

[ Thursday the 20:] 

After priuat praers, I did eat my breakfast : then I wrett in my

went about the house and, in talke with Mr Rhodes, I under- 
Stood thus much, that, wher as graces are of 2 sortes, some generall, 
belonginge to euerie christian, others speciall, as Contenancie 
and . . .271 for the better preuailinge against a temptation, it 
is not sufficient only to haue faith, wherby I know that I shall 
nether yeald ynto it, nor be ouer come by it : but I must Likewise 
pray especially for that vertue which is opposed to that vise wher 
vnto I am then tempted, because, though faith be the Fondamentall 
Cause of ouercoming sinne, yet oppretiuely the seuerall graces of 
god work: after, I supped, then I hard the Le¢tor, and then 
praied, and so went to bed

the lordes day after, which was the 9 of March I durst not

by that means depriued of the word and Sacrementes: and, 
though I know the Lord is powerfull And hath promised 
to keep his Children without the meanes, when he doth not 
afford them vnto them, yet, when he depriueth them of the 
ordenarie Instrumentes wherby he hath promised to Conuaye his 
graces vnto His people for their sinnes, there is great Cause of

did denie, Could not be saued: the one was [ustefecation by 
and in christes Righteousnes :_ the other, of life touchinge the true

I was to vndertake, nor Throughly taught touchinge theparuartinge 
the ende of witnesses from a christiane instetutione . . . sluell 
pollicie : but I will inquire more of this matter, god willinge, with 
the next faithfull deuine, beinge loth to denie, if I may, any freind 
such a Curtesie : after, I went about the house and took order for 
supper, and after returned to priuat examenation and praier : 
after, I went to supper, then I reed, and lastly went to bed :

I hard Mr Rhodes read, and wrought with in a while: after, 
I went to see a calfe at Munckmans, which had :2: great heades, 
4 eares, and had to ether head a throte pipe besides :_ the heades 
had longe heares like brissels about the mouths, such as n’other 
Cowe hath: the hinder legges had no parting from the rumpe, 
but grewe backward, and were no longer but from the first 
Ioynte : also, the backe bone was parted about the midest bicke, 
and a rowne howle was in the midest into the bodie of the Calfe : 
but one would haue thought that to haue comed of some strocke 
it might gett in the Cowes bely : after this I Came in to priuat 
medetation and praier

? ‘This transaction gave rise later to a law-suit in Chancery. When 
he died, Mr. Dakins had not fully paid up to the trustees his share of 
the purchase-money of the property of his son-in-law and daughter. 
Soon afterwards Margaret’s first husband also died. The Earl of 
Huntingdon, Devereux’s uncle, paid up the rest of the purchase-money 
for the estate, and married Margaret to another nephew. Shortly 
afterwards, this second husband died, also without paying off any of their 
debt on the estate. When Margaret married a third time, the new Earl 
of Huntingdon, who had succeeded to his brother’s title and property, 
claimed as his own the manor of Hackness. There were no children of 
the marriages. Margaret, who by her third marriage had become 
Lady Hoby, fought, together with her husband, in the Court of Chancery 
for the property. Her diary records the successful issue of their suit. 
(See Fortescue Papers, ed. Gardiner, S. R. (Cam. Soc., 1871), pp. xxii, 
et seq., where is printed Chancery Orders and Decrees, 1597 A. f. 88. 
See also at the Record Office Eliz., Chancery Decrees, iii, Case no. 6,
