231 


232 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


between the families of Eure and Dakins. In 1536 Ralph Eure accused 
Dakins of assisting to murder his, Eure’s, servant, and complained 
bitterly because Dakins, having influence in high places, went scot-free. 
The ecclesiastical lands in which George Dakins speculated had once 
belonged to Old Malton priory. It was at Malton that the Eures lived, 
for they were lords of the manor and still remained secretly loyal to the 
old faith in Elizabeth’s day. ‘The enmity between the two houses 
seems to have persisted and was made much of in evidence given before 
the Court of Star Chamber to which reference will be made later. (Star 
Chamber 5, Proceedings 43 Eliz., Bundle 22, no. 21. ‘This resulted 
from events recorded in the diary for 27th August, 1600. See also 
notes thereon.)

108 Tbid., p. 33. Sir Hugh, the author of the Memoirs, continued : 
“and within a year, being at the sessions, and differing in opinion with 
him, he thought to put an affront on me, by determining the matter after 
his own mind; but I, putting it to the opinion of the bench, had them all 
on my side, insomuch as he, turning about, said to the gentleman that 
sat next to him, ‘ His grandfather once crossed me thus on the bench, 
but I made him repent it; and so will I this man.’ And truly soon 
after he began a suit against me in the Star Chamber, and I one against 
him in the Court of York; but, finding himself to have the worst end of 
the staff, procured the Lord Coventry, then Lord Keeper, to send for 
me to compose both; but, after the check I gave him in the Sessions, 
he never appeared there more, nor was so active or formidable in the 
country as formerly. The Sydenhams, now possessed of Hackness, 
may in some sort thank me for it: for Sir Thomas Hobby, to make the 
Lord Coventry his friend against me in the difference, proposed his cousin 
Sydenham in marriage to my Lord’s grandchild, and so settled Hackness 
on him, which in right belonged to Mr. Dakins, next to Sir Thomas’s 
lady, whose land that was.” 

109 Salisbury MSS., pt. xi, p. 393; roth Feb., r60or. Until after 
this time Henry Cholmley and his wife were supporters of the Roman 
Catholic cause in England. ‘Their grandson Hugh thus describes them : 
“His wife . . . was a Roman Catholic, and he living then at Whitby, 
it was a receptacle to the seminary priests coming from beyond seas, and 
landing frequently at that port; insomuch as, I have been told, there 
have been in his house three or four of them together at a time, and, 
most coming both bare of cloaths and money, have, at his lady’s charge, 
been sent away with a very great supply of both; some in scarlet and 
sattin, with their men and horses, the better to disguise their professions. 
All which Sir Henry connived at, being a little then in his heart inclining 
that way, though he went to church. And as the prosecution of Papists 
was then severe, so was he put to much trouble and charge for his Lady, 
not only in respect to compositions, but that she was often carried to,

and kept long in prison, as were most of the eminent Papists in those

Protestants”. (Ise Memoirs of Sir Hugh Cholmley, pp. 10-11 3 1870).

130 Quarlous, in Jonson’s Bartholomew Fair (1, 1) draws a vivid picture 
for the benefit of his friend, about to wed a rich old widow of Puritanical 
persuasion. “* Dost thou ever think to bring thine ears or stomach to 
the patience of a dry grace as long as thy table-cloth ; and droned out by 
thy son here . . . till all the meat on thy board has forgot it was that day 
in the kitchen ? or to brook the noise made in a question of predestination, 
by the good labourers and painful eaters assembled together, put to them 
by the matron your spouse ; who moderates with a cup of wine, ever and 
anon, and a sentence out of Knox between? Or the perpetual spitting 
before and after a sober-drawn exhortation of six hours, whose better 
part was the hum-ha-hum ? or to hear prayers groaned out over thy iron 
chests, as if they were charms to break them? And all this for the hope 
of two apostle-spoons, to suffer! and a cup to eat a caudle in ! for that will 
be thy legacy. She’ll have convey’d her state safe enough from thee, 
an she be a right widow.” 

The Puritan was hostile to every form of pleasure since such was 
regarded as tending to withdraw the mind from the contemplation of 
things spiritual, thus jeopardizing the soul’s salvation. ‘There must 
be no indulgence, either of self or others. Excellently is this illustrated 
in the life and death of Katherine Stubbes, the wife of the moralist. She 
died young, in 1590, and, soon after, her husband wrote a little memoir 
of her virtues and piety and her mode of living, that she might serve for 
ever as an example to others. He called it “A Christall Glas, for 
Christian women: wherein they may see a wonderfull and true example 
of a right vertuous life and Christian death : as by the discourse following, 
to their further instruction and comfort, it may appear”. Mistress 
Stubbes was married at fifteen and died before she was twenty. Her 
husband records, among her other virtues, that ‘‘ she obeied the command- 
ment of the Apostle, who biddeth women to be silent, and to learne of 
their husbands at home”. According to the ideals of the period, she 
was in every way a model wife, never quarrelling, never going out when 
her husband was away, sober, humble, taking neither delicate food nor 
strong drink, never swearing, a good housekeeper, loyal to her husband, 
merry when he was merry, sad when he was sad, never crossing him but 
rather persuading him with wise advice. One anecdote in particular 


240 DIARY OFSEA DY 7H OB Y, 


illustrates her Puritanism. On her death-bed she noticed, lying at her 
feet, a little dog of which she was very fond. She drove her away, saying 
to her husband, “ Good husband, you and I have offended God 
grievously in receiving this bitch many a time into our bed; the Lord 
give me grace to repent it and all other vanities.”

at a Vestry meeting of the parishioners of St. Margaret Lothbury, held 
27th July, 1573, it was “ Agred by the consent of Mr parsone and the 
parrisheners ...to have a Le&tor Every wendensday and Friday beginninge 
att 5 A cloke and Endinge att 6 Acloke, the bell to towell halfe anower

180 "This man, Richard Rhodes, is very often mentioned by Lady Hoby. 
His office in the household is difficult to define, for there is no direct 
evidence that he was resident therein, though he would seem, according 
to the diary, to have performed the duties of a chaplain. Moreover, 
Lady Hoby’s allusions to him show that, at any rate in the months covered 
by the first part of the diary, he frequently accompanied her when she 
went away to stay with friends or relatives. An explanation of his 
presence may lie in the fact that in England at this period, as a corre- 
spondent told Lord Burghley, you might have, in any large house, “ at 
your pleasure from Cambridge some one that for lack of exhibition would 
be glad for a year or two to do service there daily.” (Salisbury MSS., 
Hist. MSS. Com., Pt. ii, p. §2.) ‘The first mention of Rhodes in the 
parish register of Hackness is on 8th January, 1601, when Rebecca, 
wife of Richard Rhodes, was buried there, and in the diary, in June of 
that same year, there is a reference to his house. But at this 
time, August, 1599, William Ashton was the parish priest, and as 


244 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


such signed the parish register with the churchwardens. Early in 1603 
Rhodes married a second time, his wife being a girl of good family in 
attendance on Lady Hoby. As Lupton wrote of country chaplains, 
in London and the Country Carbonadoed: “If they come single, its a 
thousand to one but they will either be in Love or Married before they 
goe away.” (Quoted by Fox, E., op. cit., p. 160.) At the baptism of 
his daughter, Peace, in May, 1604, Rhodes was designated in the parish 
register, “ Preacher of God’s word at Hackness,” but not until 1605 
did he himself sign the register, with the churchwardens, as parish priest. 
One Richard Rodes, layman, appears as a licensed reader in the diocese 
of Lincoln in 1597, and later as the incumbent of Burwell, though still 
only a reader. (The State of the Church, temp. Eliz. and Fames I, ed. 
Foster, C. W., pp. xxxiii, 322,411,426. (Lincoln Record Society) 1926.) 
He may have been a connection of a friend of the Hoby family, 
Godfrey Rhodes, esq., of Great Houghton, Yorks.: but in the Hackness 
Register there is recorded, in 1591, the baptism of Elizabeth Rhodes, 
daughter of one Andrew Rhodes, so that his family may, after all, have 
belonged to the immediate vicinity of Hackness. 

181 This religious exercise, wrote John Manningham in his Diary 
three years later, was defined by Stephen Egerton, a very popular Puritan 
divine in London, “to be a breife and familiar kinde of teaching the 
principles of relligion, in a plaine manner, by way of question and aunswere, 
either publiquely by the minister, or privately by the maister or mistres 
of the family.” (Manningham’s Diary (Camden Society, 1868), pp. 
IOI-2).

183 "This was frequently the benevolent duty of the lady of the manor, 
who usually knew more of surgery and medicine than anyone in the 
village. The acquirement of such knowledge was an essential and 
traditional part of the upbringing of girls of wealth and rank in the Tudor 
period. No doétor was, as a rule, available in the country, and the 
whole district depended upon her ministrations, as, in the case of Lady 
Hoby, later entries show, or upon travelling quacks or local 
herbwomen.

and catechizing, was Lady Hoby’s method of training her household in

189 "This appears to be Lady Hoby’s usual hour for breakfast. At 
this meal she would use a knife and spoon, as forks did not come into use 
until the seventeenth century. ‘The items of food and drink of which 
she partook would be any one or more of the following: bread, butter, 
beer, wine, mutton, beef, veal, rabbit, and chicken. (Shakespeare’s 
England, vol. ii, p. 134. 1916). 

190 Here Lady Hoby uses the word “ lector’ to mean a passage to be 
read or perused, or even the action of reading or perusal. She meant 
perhaps to imply an hour or more of study with Mr. Rhodes. 

191 "This was the most important meal of the day and was usually 
served between eleven and twelve o’clock. Apparently every kind of 
food with which we are familiar was served—fresh- and salt-water fish, 
oysters, all kinds of game, vegetables, and fruit. Merchants and gentle- 
men kept similar tables and seem to have been content with from one to 
three dishes when alone, and from four to six when entertaining guests. 
With the general increase in prosperity appeared a corresponding 
extravagance in food and elaborate cooking with eccentric flavours 
became popular among the rich, while luxuries were very dear, and 
enormous quantities of meat were consumed. (Shakespeare’s England, 
loc. cit.)

all the small country places are thus designated.

it was, generally speaking, a modified version of dinner (Shakespeare’s

196 ‘The first coach in England was made in 1555 for the Earl of 
Rutland by the same man who made one for Queen Mary in the following 
year and a State-coach for Elizabeth in 1564. One of the carriages 
which the latter used could be opened and closed at will. Not until about 
1580 were coaches of the type now properly so called used in England. 
‘They were springless affairs but they became so popular that, in 1601, 
a bill was introduced into Parliament “ to restrain the excessive use of 
coaches”. It was estimated that, early in the seventeenth century, 
there were more than six thousand in London and its neighbourhood.

The ‘Thames watermen suffered severely from the competition, and 
Taylor, their poet, thus complained : 


“* Carroaches, coaches, jades, and Flanders mares 
Doe rob us of our shares, our wares, our fares. 
Against the ground we stand and knock our heels, 
Whilst all our profit runs away on wheeles.” 
(Art. on Carriage, Encyclop. Britt, 11th edn.)

198 [he Puritans were remarkable for their diligent attendance at 
sermons. ‘They regarded preaching as one of the most important of 
a priest’s duties, and constantly inveighed against its negle&t by the Church. 
As early as 1574 the fashion had reached the circle about the Queen, 
and it was remarked that “ The Court has great plenty of ladies to hear 
sermons.” (Rutland MSS., Hist. MSS. Com., Rept. xii, App. iv, p. ror.) 
The great vogue of the sermon was indeed a prominent chara¢teristic 
of Elizabethan and Jacobean religion, a faét which is remarkably well 
illustrated in the diary of John Manningham of the Middle Temple, 
a young man not specially pious, and in this of the more religious Lady 
Hoby. Manningham’s record contains summaries, a few of which are 
quite lengthy, of more than forty sermons. In 1588 the deliberations 
of the Mickleton Jury of the town of Nottingham resulted in a message 
to the Mayor and his brethren that, since the service of Almighty God 
was most carefully to be considered before all worldly matters, “‘ we have 
thought good, for as much as we have a most godly exercise of preaching 
on the Friday once a week, and lest the same should decay among us 
through our negligence in not coming as we ought to do, and specially 
of the cheefest of our town, which ought to be most present, it shall be 
very good, not only for yourself with the rest of your brethren, but also 
that the whole Council with the Clothing of this town, most reverently 
to observe the same time by some special order set down by you, and that 
the same be duly kept.” (Nottingham Borough Records, vol. iv, p. 222.) 
In 1617 the Town Council was paying £10 a year as salary to a preacher 
‘in regard of his great pains which he continually taketh; and the same 
to continue further and longer time if he stay in the town”. ‘These 
preachers were usually Puritans. (Ibid., pp. 354-5.) 

199 Tt is not clear whether Lady Hoby here means their spiritual or 
material “‘ estates”. It is probably the latter, and this visit to her mother 
may have been upon family business. For the law-suits in which her 
inheritance had involved them, see the Introdu¢tion. 

200 ‘This, according to the Injunétions issued by the Queen at the 
beginning of her reign, was the duty of every parish priest. At the 


NOTES 247 


visitation of the province of York by Archbishop Grindal in 1571, two 
of the articles to be inquired of in every parish were as follows: “‘ Whether 
your parson, vicar, or curate do every Sunday and Holy Day openly in 
the church, call for, hear, and instrudt all the children, apprentices, and 
servants of both sexes, that be of convenient age within your parish, with 
at the least so many of them by course as the time will serve, and as he may 
well hear and instruct, for half an hour at the least before Evening Prayer, 
in the Ten Commandments, the Articles of the belief, and the Lord’s 
Prayer, and diligently examine and teach them the Catechism set forth 
in the book of Common Prayer, and whether for that purpose he doth 
take the names of them all, and by course call certain of them by name 
every Sunday and Holy Day to come to the teaching of the same 
Catechism ?” 

“Whether all fathers and mothers, masters, and dames of 
your parish cause their children, servants and apprentices, both mankind 
and womankind, being above seven years of age and under twenty, 
which have not learned the Catechism, to come to the church on Sundays 
and Holy Days at the time appointed, or at the least such and so many 
of them as your minister shall appoint, and there diligently and obediently 
to hear and to be ordered by the minister until such time as they have 
learned the same Catechism.” ‘The article proceeds to inquire the names 
of those who do not thus look to the spiritual welfare of their households 
and families, and the identity of those under twenty who are still ignorant 
of the Catechism. Succeeding Archbishops instituted similar queries, 
for the province of York was full of recusants. Grindal told Cecil, 
in 1571, that it was “not well affected to godly religion: among the 
people there are many remnants of the old. They keep Holy Days and 
fasts abrogated: they offer money, eggs, etc., at the burial of their dead : 
they pray on beads, etc., so it seemeth to be, as it were, another church, 
rather than a member of the rest”? (Kennedy, W. M. C., and Frere, 
W.#HL., Visitation Articles and Injunctions of the Period of the Reformation, 
Alcuin Club Collections, vol. xvi, pp. 258-9, 253. Ig10.

202 Tt is not possible to say to which of the books of Thomas Cartwright 
Lady Hoby refers here. Her mother-in-law, the Lady Elizabeth Russell, 
and the latter’s sisters, were supporters of this learned Puritan scholar 
and eloquent, influential teacher, as were other members of the families 
with which Lady Hoby was connected; e.g. Robert, Earl of Leicester, 
Nathaniel Bacon, Sir Francis Walsingham, and Ambrose, Earl of Warwick. 
Cartwright died in 1603, having given in his life-time, by his teaching, 
both system and method to the puritanism of the time. (See article on 
him in Did. of Nat. Biography. 1908.) 

203 The sense in which Lady Hoby uses this word may differ from 
that with which we are familiar. Amongst “ Certain orders for the 


248 DIARY) OFT EA Diya nH Oy, 


increase of learning in the unlearned sort of ministers ” (c. 1585) is the 
following: “The ordinarie of the place shall likewise appointe vnto 
them everie quarter of a yere a common place of Diuinitie to be written 
vpon in Latten by theim, and to be travelled in, so as they be hable to 
aunswer to the principall pointes thereof in Latten.” (Printed from 
Cardwell, Documentary Annals, il, pp. 21, 22, by Foster, C. W., The 
State of the Church, temp. Eliz. and F. I (Lincoln Record Society), 
p. xx. 1926). A common-place book was, originally, one in which 
“commonplaces ”’, or passages important for reference, were collected, 
usually under general heads; hence it came to be one in which were 
recorded, with or without arrangement, passages or matters to be especially 
remembered or referred to. Doubtless Lady Hoby’s book was arranged 
under general heads, and her extracts probably culled from sermons. 
As Marston wrote, in The Scourge of Villanie (III, ii, 226): “ Now I 
haue him, that ... Hath made a common-place booke out of playes, and 
speaks in print.” (Commonplace in New Engl. Did.)

207 Sermons vppon the Songe of Salomon by Master George Gifforde 
was entered in the Stationers’ Register, 21st March [1598]. (4 
Transcript of the Registers of the Company of Stationers of London, ed. 
Arber, E., vol. i, p. 324. 1876.) Educated both at Oxford and Cam- 
bridge, the author was a Puritan divine, well-known for his preaching. 
For his opinions and refusal to subscribe to the Thirty-Nine Articles of 
the Established Church, he was deprived of his living of All Saints’ with 
St. Peter’s, at Maldon, Essex, in 1584, and tried before the Court of 
High Commission. His parishioners petitioned for his restoration, 
because of the reformation worked amongst them by his teaching, and he 
was allowed to continue to preach by holding the office of lecturer at 
Maldon. From this he was suspended in 1587, but nevertheless con- 
tinued to preach to the end. He died in 1620. ‘The book to which 
Lady Hoby refers was dedicated by its author to the Earl of Essex, her 
brother-in-law.

210 ’This was probably a ““ book ” of the pews in the Church. Disputes

211 Richard Greenham wasa learned Puritan divine, active and zealous, 
a Fellow of Pembroke Hall,Cambridge, in 1564, and rector of Dry Drayton, 
1570-91. He was cited by Bishop Cox for non-conformity and was one 
of the eighteen influential members of the University who signed a 
memorial, sent to Cecil in 1570, testifying to the good character and 
attainments of Cartwright. ‘The latter was, at that time, Lady Margaret 
professor in the University, which he was stirring to its depths by his 
denunciations of the constitution of the church. Greenham is thought 
to have died ¢. 1594. His Works were edited by Henry Holland and 
published, in four parts, in 1599. (D.N.B.: Art. Richard Greenham.) 
The Short Title Catalogue of the Bibliographical Society notices four 
editions of his Works by 1605.

218 "This was probably the New herba// of William Turner the reformer, 
who, having a garden at Kew, indulged in botany as a relief from ardent 
protestantism. His 4erda// is considered to mark the beginning of the 
scientific study of botany in England. Numerous books of this class 
appeared in the Tudor period, one of the earliest being the Grete Herbal/, 
printed by Peter Treveris at Southwark in 1526, and founded upon 
the French Grand Herbier. Four editions of this Grete Herball had 
appeared by 1561. The Niewe herbal/ of Rembert Dodoens, translated 
into English by Henry Lyte in 1578, was also very popular, as was the 
Herball or Generall Historie of Plantes of John Gerard, produced in 1597. 

Turner’s 4erbal/ appeared first in 1551, then, with a second part and 
A booke of the natures of the bathes in England, etc., in 1562. A folio 
edition of the book in 1568 included four parts. Lady Hoby’s “ arball ” 
might even have been the newer one of Gerard. (Cam. Hist. Engl. 
Lit., iv, pp. 374-5; Short-Title Catalogue (Bibliog. Soc. Publications), 
1926.) Hakluyt, in the Principal Navigations, recommended that 
those seeking the North-East passage should take with them “ the new 
Herball and such bookes as make shew of herbes, plants, trees, fishes, 
foules, and beasts of these regions ”’, for “it may much delight the great 
Can, and the nobilitie, and also their merchants to have a view of them.” 
(Principal Navigations, ill, p. 265; printed in Tawnay and Power, 
Tudor Economic Documents, iii, p. 237.) 

Gerard’s Herba// included, under a description “ of Indian Swallow 
woort ”, a contemporaneous notice of Sir Walter Raleigh’s Virginia 
colony of 1585.

#32 Tt seems impossible to exaggerate the influence of this book on 
English thought and feeling. During Mary’s reign Foxe was an exile 
at Basle, and it was there that the scheme of producing a history of the 
Protestant victims of the persecution in England was born. It was to be 
set forth both in Latin and English, and Grindal and Aylmer, fellow- 
exiles in Germany, offered their help. When Mary died, they returned 
to London and hurried forward the English version of the work, which 
was printed, in March, 1563, in folio, by John Day, as the 4dées and 
Monuments of these latter and perilous Dayes. An excellent series of 
wood-cuts formed the illustrations. Convocation ordered copies to be 
placed in all cathedral churches, and many were bought for parish churches. 
The Roman Catholics designated it “ Foxe’s Golden Legend”, which 
really admirably reveals the place it occupied in people’s minds, for it 
quite superseded that mediaeval collection of the acts of saints and martyrs 
of which the Reformation had deprived them. ‘The two folio volumes 
of the second edition of 1570 consisted of 934 and 1,378 pages respectively. 
By 1596 five editions had been printed. ‘The book was universally 
read and roused a storm of argument when it was first published. Foxe 
was a violent partisan, content only with universality and exhaustiveness. 
Hence he began with descriptions of the martyrs of the early church, 
and of those who suffered in England under Lucius. Incidentally, he 
much despised Becket, because, since he was the open and avowed friend 
of the Pope, he was, in Foxe’s eyes, no true martyr. But it is when he 
reaches the Tudor period that he excels, and waxes enthusiastic over the 
prowess of Henry VIII, the achievements of Cromwell, and the 
magnanimity of Anne Boleyn, “‘ who, without controversy, was a special 


NOTES 253 


comforter and aider of all the professors of Christ’s gospel.” ‘To Foxe, 
only those are martyrs who died in the Protestant cause, and he spares 
us nothing of their torments. No good could come from the other side, 
and he pictures Sir Thomas More as “a bitter persecutor of good men 
and a wretched enemy against the truth of the gospel.” 

The style of the book is sufficient to account for its immediate and 
prolonged popularity, for it holds our interest by its mixture of gossip, 
detail, document, and exhortation, by the dramatic turn given to its 
dialogues, and by the reality of its heroes and heroines. (John Foxe, 
Adis and Monuments, ed. G. Townsend and S. R. Cattley, 8 vols., 1841, 
etc.; Camb. Hist. of Engl. Lit.; D.N.B., art, Fohn Foxe; Shake- 
peare’s England, vol.i, pp. 70-1; Sthort-Title Catalogue (Bibliog. Soc.), 
1926.) So popular was Foxe’s great work that an abridgement of it 
was made by ‘Timothy Bright in 1589 and issued in quarto, “‘ for the 
behoof of such as either through want of leisure to peruse, or 
ability to buy, those great volumes have no use of that notable 
history.” ‘The Bishop of London informed the Archdeacon of 
St. Albans that the Archbishop of Canterbury had written to 
him as follows of this production and its author: “that his book 
so abridged, and containing briefly the effect of all the: histories of 
this Church, and the worthy constancy of all the martyrs in testimony 
of the truth, is very behoofful to be bought of most sort of men that are 
able. It is but small in price yet of great worth, and the author 
himself of great sufficiency in good learning, and worthy to be 
encouraged. ‘Therefore, as one most willing to set forward her Majesty’s 
gracious disposition towards him, with the profitting of as many as shall 
be willing with the expense but of 5s. for every several book, ready bound 
up in paste and clasped, to buy so great good to themselves, I have thought 
it good to make known to your Lordship mine opinion of him, and of 
his said books, desiring your Lordship by your Chancellor, Archdeacons, 
etc., when opportunity shall offer, to recommend the same to the Clergy 
and Laity in your Diocese. And that he may be certified how many 
shall be needful at any one time to be sent, and such as dwell far off and 
are willing to buy them at the price abovesaid, be not troubled to fetch 
them, I would wish that such as will have any of them should set down 
their names with your Lordship’s Registrar or Archdeacon, and they with 
convenient speed by their letters to certify Dr. Brighte himself, 
at St. Bartholomew’s by Smithfield in London, or Mr. Hartewell my 
Secretary. Your Lordship is prayed to signify whom you have appointed 
in your Diocese to receive the books when they shall be sent, and return 
the money to Dr. Brighte.” ‘The Bishop proceeded then to desire his 
Archdeacon, since he thought good would come by it to the Church, 
to persuade most earnestly each minister and preacher to buy “ into 
every theire houses” a copy of the book for the use of their families, 


254 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


while in every parish the Churchwardens were to “ be drawn to have 
one of them to place in their church for such as shall come into the church 
to read upon; And for the inferior sort of Ministers who be no Preachers, 
or such as be not public Preachers, they should be urged to have one of 
them to exercise themselves therein, both to their own benefit, and the 
benefit of their charges.” (Records of the Old Archdeanery of St. Albans, 
ed. H. R. Wilton Hall, p.72. (St. Albans and Herts Architec. and 
Archzol. Soc.) 1908.) With such a backing, it was no wonder that 
Foxe’s Book of Martyrs, or Timothy Bright’s abridgement of it, were 
well known.

#40 Gervase Babington (1550-1610) was, by this date, the author 
of many theological treatises and sermons, though his works were not 
collected and published as a whole until 1615. Of his 4 drief conference 
betwixt mans frailtie and faith, published in 1583, there were four more 
editions by 1602. ‘This was in dialogue form and dedicated by the 
author to Mary Sidney, Countess of Pembroke, Lady Hoby’s sister-in-law. 
Certaine plaine, briefe, and comfortable notes upon euerie chapter of Genesis, 
published in 1592, was printed again, in an enlarged edition, in 1596. 
From 4 very fruitfull exposition of the Commaundements, first printed in 
1583, and again in 1586, 1590, and 1596, it is possible to form an idea 
of the principles imbibed by Lady Hoby from her reading. Of the 
drama, Babington wrote: “These prophane and wanton stage plays 
or interludes, what an occasion they are of adultery and uncleanness... 
the world knoweth with too much hurt by long experience. . . These 
players behaviour polluteth all things. And of their plays he [Chrysostom] 
saith: they are the feasts of Satan, the inventions of the devill, etc... 
Polluted bodies by these filthy occasions have on their death beds confesed 
the danger of them.” (Printed in Sheavyn, P., Te Literary Profession 
in the Elizabethan Age, p. 173. (1909.)) Babington was, in succession, 
Bishop of Llandaff, of Exeter, and of Worcester. He was educated 
at Cambridge, and afterwards received into the household of Mary 
Sidney and her husband, the Earl of Pembroke, as tutor to their two sons, 
to whom he later dedicated his collefted works. He is said to have 


NOTES 255 


assisted the Countess with her versification of the Psalms. He was a 
constant preacher and a great reader, knowing both Greek and Hebrew. 
He was elected Bishop of Llandaff in 1591 largely through his patron’s 
influence. Many of his books, in his own quaint and pleasant style, 
were published in little quartos, as also were several of his sermons at 
St. Paul’s Cross. He was one of those summoned to the Hampton 
Court Conferencein 1604. (S4ort-Title Catalogue (Bibliog. Soc.), 1926 ; 
D.N.B., art., Gervase Babington.) 

241 ‘The word is spelt thus in the original, for Lady Hoby divided 
the word at the end of a line, Mallinco being one part and colie 
the other. This book was 4 treatise of melancholie, containing the 
causes thereof, printed by ‘I’. Vautrollier in 1586, another edition being 
issued in the same year. We have already referred to the author as 
having made the Abridgment of Foxe’s Book of Martyrs. ‘Timothy 
Bright was educated at Cambridge, and then studied medicine in Paris, 
later becoming physician at “ Bart’s” from 1586 to 1590. He then 
gave up the medical profession, and was presented by Elizabeth to the 
living of Methley in Yorkshire in 1591, and of Berwick in Elmet, in 
addition, in 1594. 

His first medical work, in two parts, published in 1584, Hygieiza and 
Therapeutica, was dedicated to Lord Burghley. In it Bright praised 
the learning of Lady Burghley (Lady Hoby’s aunt by marriage) and 
compared to a university the “ domus Ceciliana”’. “The book to which 
Lady Hoby refers in the text was as much metaphysical as medical. 
In one chapter, Bright discusses “‘ how the soule, by one simple faculty, 
performeth so many and diverse actions ”’, illustrating his argument by 
describing the way in which the complicated movements of a watch 
proceed “ from one right and straight motion”. He is, however, best 
known as the inventor of modern shorthand. His Charaderie, published 
in 1588, was a work on this lost art which he re-discovered. He 
dedicated his book to the Queen, and, in reward, was granted the York- 
shire rectories and letters patent to print all such books as he should 
“devise or abridge”. We have already quoted the words in which 
the Bishop of London advertised the excellence of his 4bridgment of the 
Book of Martyrs. (Short-Title Catalogue, 1926; D.N.B., art. Timothy 
Bright).

257 Much responsibility rested upon the head of a large household 
who had, in her care, several youths and girls of good family, all receiving 
their education through this form of service in a household similar to 
that of the families whence they came. The moral standards of the 
period were very low: it must be realized that many of the Queen’s 
Maids of Honour were with child before their marriage. 

258 Metrical versions of the Psalms were exceedingly popular at this 
time. John Day issued many editions from 1562 onwards, for he had 
the monopoly of printing what became known as the Old Version, 
the title-page of which explains its use. “*‘’The whole booke of Psalmes 
newly set forth and .. . allowed to be song in all Churches of all the 
people together, before and after morning and evening prayer: as also 


NOTES 257 


before and after the Sermon; and, moreover, in private houses for their 
godly solace and comfort, laying aparte all ungodly songes and balades.” 
Of the psalms in the Old Version only one has remained in use to this 
day: it is William Kethe’s “ All people that on earth do well”. Many 
people were encouraged by this vogue of the metrical psalm to make 
translations of their own: Queen Elizabeth and Edmund Spenser 
were amongst those who produced versions of selected psalms. Sir 
Philip Sidney began a new translation of the whole body of the psalms 
which was finished by his sister Mary, Countess of Pembroke. (Shake- 
speare’s England, i, p. 73.) 

The Puritans were greatly addicted to private psalm-singing. Ben 
Jonson makes ‘Truewit tell Morose in The Silent Woman (11, i) that, 
if he marry one who is “ precise ”, he must be content to listen to “ long- 
winded exercises, singings, and catechisings ”’.

262 William Perkins (1558-1603), a Puritan theologian, writer, and 
preacher, was born in Warwickshire, and educated at Cambridge. Here, 
doubtless, he acquired his Puritan bias. Elected a Fellow of Christ’s 
College, he took the Puritan side in debates between that party and the 
Anglicans, and was always emphatically against the distinctive tenets 
of the Roman Church. His powers as a preacher soon made him famous. 
In 1589 a synod, which Perkins attended, met at St. John’s College 
to revise the treatise Of Discipline, known later as The Diredory, and 
important as an epitome of Puritan doétrine. Able, earnest, and sound 
in judgment, Perkins was unrivalled, in the later years of his life, as a 
teacher in the University. His influence was enormous, for he appealed 
both to the University and, through his writings, to a wider public ; 
to the one by bringing to bear, upon questions of divinity, the scholarship 
and method of the University ; to the other by abandoning the abstruse 
and unprofitable stuff usually discussed, and discoursing, in simple fashion, 
upon the main do¢trines of Christian theology. ‘This he defined as 
“the science of living blessedly for ever”. Perkins never separated 
from the Established Church; he was a forceful antagonist of the 
prevalent belief in astrology, and thought all atheists should be put to 
death. Such was the theologian whose books Lady Hoby read. ‘To 
which of these she refers here it is impossible to say, for Perkins was 


S) 


258 DIARY NO PRL ADD EO. Bex 


a prolific writer, and remarkably popular. Proof of this last lies in the 
number of languages into which many of his works were translated— 
Latin, Dutch, Spanish, Welsh, and Irish—and the number of times his 
heavy tomes were reprinted. Not, apparently, until 1600, were his 
writings gathered together and printed, as a whole, by the printer to the 
University, under the title of 4 Golden Chaine: or the description of 
theologie, with twelve other treatises. ‘This made a quarto of 1,057 
pages. In 1603 came a second edition, containing five more parts, 
and others followed, until 1635. Perkins was regarded in the 
seventeenth century as a writer almost equal in authority to Hooker 
or Calvin. 

His works included treatises entitled 4 direction for the gouernment of 
the tongue, An exposition of the Lords Prayer, and An exposition of the 
symbole or creed of the apostles, etc. In this last, Perkins attacked the 
doétrine of the descent into hell, and in 4 reformed Catholike (1598) 
he endeavoured to draw a line which would indicate the essential points 


of difference between the Protestant and Roman churches and their ° 


teachings, and beyond which it seemed to him impossible for the reformed 
church to go. ‘This book was an important contribution to the con- 
troversy of the period. (D.N.B.: art. William Perkins; S4ort-Tizle 
Catalogue (Bibliog. Soc.), 1926).

282 Lady Hoby probably refers here to the work of Thomas Bilson, 
Bishop of Winchester, who published in 1599 The effed of certaine 
sermons, touching the full Redemption of Mankind. In a letter written 
from London on 28th June, 1599, John Chamberlain noted: “The 
Bishop of Winchester hath printed his two sermons, about which there 
was so much Stirre two or three yeares ago.” (Letters written by Fohn 
Chamberlain, ed. Williams, S. (Cam. Soc., 1861), p. 53.) Bilson had 
previously written several treatises including, in 1593, 4 compendious 
discourse prouing episcopacy to be of divine institution, and The perpetual 
government of Christes church. His views were, of course, opposed 
to those of the Puritans. Bilson was translated from Worcester to 
Winchester in 1597, and died in 1616.

309 That parents should choose their children’s partners in marriage 
was a principle firmly held by the Puritans, as, indeed, by most 
of ‘Tudor England, at this period. It was a reasonable view, in face 
of the early espousals that were customary. It is, however, noteworthy 
that the subject was already a debatable one. Evidence of this is abundant 
in the more serious literature of the time, where the topic is frequently 
discussed. (See, especially, Te Works of Thomas Becon, ed. Ayre, J. 
(Parker Society), 1844.)

$14 The instructions addressed by the Queen to Thomas Cecil when 
he was made President of the Council of the North, in the summer of 
1599, give a most curious account of the condition of Yorkshire at the time, 
and of the widespread discontent prevailing there. Lord Burghley 
was charged to resort to strong measures to reduce the recusant gentry 
to obedience and to hunt down the papists and all the priests. He was 
not at all reluctant to obey orders. (D.N.B., art. on Cecil, Thomas, 
second Lord Burghley.) In 1591 the Archbishop of York, Piers, and 
his bishops, agreed upon certain orders to be observed for the government 
of the church in the province, the sixth of which was: “ all recusants and 
especially those of the best sort to be proceeded against effectually and 
speedily, so far as the law will yield power.” (Elizabethan Episcopal 
AdminiStration, ed. by Kennedy, W. P. M. (Alcuin Club Collections), 
vol. xxvi, p. 266.) The sparsely populated moorland between Hackness 
and Whitby, and all around, together with the lonely coast, provided 
excellent lurking places for the priests of the old religion and their 
staunch adherents. 

On rith February, 1599, Sir Thomas Hoby wrote to his cousin, 
Sir Robert Cecil, ‘‘ Give me leave to advertise you by this enclosed of 
the backwardness of our Northern parts. I have but mentioned one 
parish, but you in your wisdom will, I know, thereby measure the whole 


262 DIARY VOR MEA DY SEO Bay 


country, wherein those dangerous persons have mightily increased since 
the death of the late Lord President, and it is to be feared that a longer 
interim will daily increase them much more. ‘The place I have referred 
to, being situated along the sea coast, is of the more danger, having in it 
sundry creeks fit to receive such persons as come for evil intents, who do 
ever shun great ports. 

“P.S. Whitby Strond is a corner of Yorkshire reaching from 
Scarborough northwards upon the sea coast beyond Whitby. (Sa/ishury 
MSS., (Hist. MSS. Com.), ix, p. 68.) 

Another letter to Secretary Cecil from one of his officers, later in this 
same year, informed him: “ For two years I have kept a spy to lay a plot 
for taking Groman Abbey, Hodgson’s house, near Whitby, five miles 
from the sea, whither dangerous priests and fugitives, among which 
is David Ingleby, resort. Having been long deluded by this spy, I 
threatened to send him up to you wherevpon he has sent me word that 
the service shall be presently effeGted. ‘The house, being strong, large, 
and many conveyances underground to a brook running near, I must 
employ as many people from York as I can. Sir Thomas Hoby being 
now in London, I know of no assistance in the country, as twenty miles 
along the coast the people are wholly defected from religion and resist all 
warrants and officers that come amongst them. I resolve, though it is 
unusual, to be within four miles of the house, to prevent any rescue from 
those following Henry Cholmley, whose tenant Hodgson is. Last 
January, Aslaby, a tenant of Cholmley, rescued a recusant from a 
pursuivant of the High Commission, and forty persons armed prevented 
two men who had warrants from the Council of the North and High 
Commission to apprehend recusants; affirming that, whatever was their 
number and authority, they should be slain before any of Mr. Cholmley’s 
people should be carried away (and yet Cholmley is a justice of peace) 
and they threatened revenge against Sir Thos. Hoby. ‘This was near 
Groman Abbey ; so you see resistance and no assistance is to be expected. 

If it take effect, I may not discover place, persons, nor service to 
any here, except in general terms, and if any here take offence, I must 
rely on you. I hear David Ingleby, two priests, and the two Hodgsons, 
brothers, will be taken; also a register book or journal of all their 
treacherous practices. I beg your direction if it can come by the time, 
which is 3 May, 10a.m. ‘The place is 32 miles from York, whence 
I must send at night strength to guard the house.” Written at York, 
27th April, 1599. (8. P. Dom. Eliz., vol. cclxxvii, No. 99.) 

These letters help to explain Hoby’s unpopularity in the district.

826 Hugh Broughton (1549-1612). ‘The book to which she refers 
was, perhaps, his latest one at this date, Master Broughtons letters, 
especially his las pamphlet to and against the Archbishop of Canterbury 
about Sheol and Hades answered, 1599. "There was a new edition of 
this in 1600. Broughton was an interesting character. Educated at 
Cambridge, he attended the leCtures of Chevallier, and there laid the 
foundation of his Hebrew learning. He became a Fellow of St. John’s 
and of Christ’s, and had several well-known patrons like Sir Walter 
Mildmay and the Earls of Huntingdon and Essex. When he came to 
London he was soon known as a preacher with puritan sentiments in 
theology. For a time he lived with the Cottons, whom he infected with 
his own enthusiasm for the study of Hebrew. In 1588 was published 
his first work 4 Concent of Scripture, wherein Broughton strove to settle 
Scripture chronology and to correét profane writers by it. He held the 
absolute incorruptness of the text of both Testaments. Attacked by 
scholars, he began to hold weekly lectures in his own defence in the 
east end of St. Paul’s, and he appealed to the Queen, presenting her with 
a special copy of his book. Later, he went abroad, and, after one or two 
visits to England, remained there until the death of Elizabeth. He was 
a great theological disputant, and wrote, spoke, and published assiduously. 
He was well respected by the protagonists of other views, Jew and 
Christian alike. His great desire was to re-translate the Bible, to produce, 
with other scholars, a better version. Both Burghley and Elizabeth 
were addressed on the subject, but Broughton never obtained the means 
to carry out his great design. In 1599 he printed his Lxp/ication 
of the article respecting Christ’s descent into hell. He maintained, 
as against the Augustinian view held by most Anglican divines, that Hades 


264 DIARY (|G FR *2ADY HOBBY 


did not mean a place of torment, but the state of departed souls. It 
was Broughton who first brought this topic prominently before 
English scholars, and his name is associated with it chiefly at the 
present day. 

When James appointed fifty-four scholars to revise the translation of 
the Bible, Broughton was, to his intense disappointment, not amongst 
them. Hence the new version was criticized unsparingly by him. 
His own versions of the Prophets are chara¢terized by a majesty of 
expression which entitles them to wider recognition. (D.N.B.: art. 
Hugh Broughton.)

352 "This was probably Fruteful Sermons, the first part of which was 
printed by John Day in 1571. Parts ii and ili were published in 1575. 
This book contained the translation of Latimer’s famous sermon to 
Convocation in 1536, together with thirty-seven others. 

Hugh Latimer, the great Reformation preacher, was educated 
at Cambridge. At first he was opposed to the new religious teaching, 
but later became completely convinced of its truth, His sermons are 
representative of the first stage of the English Reformation, for 
Latimer attacked abuses in the existing Church, and pointed to the 
life and example of Christ and the lesson taught thereby, a high 
ideal of conduét. 

‘This famous teacher was natural, simple, honest and fearless, in no way 
isolated from the life around him. ‘This was the secret of his power. 
No complete sermons from his Cambridge days have survived, only 
T he tenor and effec of certain sermons made by Master Latimer in Cambridge 
about the year of our Lord 1529, between which, and those delivered 
before Edward VI twenty years later, there is no comparison. Hs 
fame rests on the discourses he preached in his old age, for “‘ he continued 
all Kyng Edwardes tyme, preaching for the most part every Sunday two 
Sermons ”’, and achieving a success never equalled again before the time 
of Whitefield and Wesley. Latimer became Bishop of Worcester, and 
then finally suffered death, under Mary, for his opinions. Preaching 
was a natural form of expression to him, but he did not concern himself 
with the publication of his sermons, which were written and set forth 
by other hands, perhaps without revision on his part. Nevertheless, 
they reveal his gifts as a debater, his ability to make immediate use of an 
interruption or unforeseen occurrence, his discursiveness, the constant 
repetition considered necessary by the teacher who would instru an 
age willing to be taught, the utterance of a bon mot while he remembered 
it, not necessarily in its best place, his plain speaking, his homely illustra- 
tions. The great art of story-telling was his, and he could point the most 


266 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


familiar Bible narrative with allusions to current events for all his illustra- 
tions were culled from court and market-place. He made “a nipping 
sermon, a rough sermon, and a sharp biting sermon ”’, aiming at “no 
great curlousness, no great clerkliness, no great affectation of words nor of 
painted eloquence”. His concern was with pra¢tical questions; his 
desire was to right social wrongs, and to expose the injustices of 
his time, to denounce not sin in the abstract but the evils of his day. 
(Camb. Hist. of Engl. Lit.) Because of this he still interests us, 
and these same characteristics may also account for his attraction for 
Lady Hoby. 

853 Her mother-in-law’s daughter by her second marriage. ‘This 
girl died, unmarried, on 2nd July, 1600. She had been one of the Maids 
of Honour and the Queen was her god-mother. She was buried in 
Westminster Abbey, the verses upon her tomb being composed by her 
learned mother. Lady Russell wrote thus to Sir Robert Cecil in January, 
1596: ‘“‘ With this beginning of the new year, I take my leave with 
praying you to tell my daughters [who were both at Court] that I am 
so busy with lawyers, that I shall have no leisure to talk with them as 
I would, therefore I would not have them hazard to take cold in coming 
to me. God bless them with the best husbands of this land, I beseech 
Christ! Your aunt, that thought on Wednesday that you should never 
have heard her speak more in faith, Elizabeth Russell, Dowager.” 
(Salisbury MSS., Hist. MSS. Com., pt. vi, p. 31.) A fortnight before 
young Elizabeth Russell’s death, her sister Ann had married Henry 
Lord Herbert, son of the Earl of Worcester. 

354 In 1598 opinion in England was sharply divided as to the 
advisability of making peace with Spain, as our ally, France, had already 
done, by the Peace of Vervins. A party in the Council, led by the aged 
Burghley, favoured a treaty with Spain: others, amongst whom was 
Essex, favoured a continuance of the war. The arguments against a 
peace just then were set forth by Essex in the form of an eloquent letter 
to Anthony Bacon. ‘This was entitled the pology of the Earl of Essex 
against? those who falsely and maliciously tax him to be the only hinderer of 
the peace and quiet of this kingdom. It was an appeal to warlike public 
opinion though it was made in the form of a personal defence. Essex 
stood high in favour with the Queen at this time and undoubtedly his 
arguments influenced her and were responsible for the postponement 
of peace for another five years. Once, when these same views were put 
forward in a private discussion, Lord Burghley quietly handed to Essex 
a copy of the psalter, pointing to the twenty-third verse of the fifty-fifth 
psalm: ‘The bloody and deceitful man shall not live out half his days. 
For a time the book circulated in manuscript : there is a copy in the British 
Museum, MS. Sloane 1132. In 1600, with Essex now in disgrace, it was 


NOTES 267 


printed, but immediately suppressed, by the Queen’s orders. A con- 
temporary wrote: “ His apologie was lately printed . . . and some few 
of them sold, but they were presently suppressed, and upon search found 
not to be don so much upon frendship or faction as upon hope of gaine.” 
(Chamberlain’s Letters (Cam. Soc., 1861), p. 76). No copy of this edition 
is known to-day, the earliest existing having been printed by R. Bradocke 
in 1603. (4 Short-Title Catalogue of English Books, Bibliog. Soc., 
1926; Cheyney, E. P., 4 History of England, ii, pp. 447 et seq., 1926), 
Robert Devereux Earl of Essex was Lady Hoby’s brother-in-law by her 
first marriage.

361 "This meeting of women after the mid-day dinner, and between 
the church services, is noteworthy. Lady Hoby must have exerted 
great influence in the neighbourhood, as did another of her contemporaries. 
Sir Hugh Cholmley wrote of his wife, whom he married in 1622, that 
“the people and country about Whitby owe a perpetual obligation to 
her memory; they and that country being much improved and refined 
by her coming thither: for as divers (and of the best in the country) 
desired to have their daughters in service with her; so being dismissed 
with many good qualities, they did communicate them to others; and 
thus not only the younger sort, and some of low rank, had improvement, 
but even the elder and best quality of housekeepers in the town, by her 
example and acquaintance, did much improve and reform both in manners 
and handsome deportment of their persons, and neatness in their houses 
and living; she being very courteous and affable to her neighbours of all 


268 DIARY OBR SDAD YeHOBRY 


ranks, and often going amongst them to visit”. She was beautiful, Sir 
Hugh recorded, both in body and mind, pious, brave, virtuous, a woman 
of discernment, and a good housekeeper. ‘“‘ She contributed much to 
beautifying of the house at Whitby, being a good contriver within doors, 
and having a most singular faculty to make and order furniture for houses, 
and dress it after the best mode, which many cannot do though they have 
stuff ; which gift she had from her mother, bred up in Queen Elizabeth’s 
court.” Everything, however, was lost during the Civil War. When 
it was over, the new blankets and bedding were supplied, without much 
expense, by Lady Cholmley’s own housewifery, and Sir Hugh makes 
special mention in his Memoirs of the green cloth hangings with flowers 
of needlework made by her and her maids, for “in her younger years, 
when first a housekeeper, she employed herself and maids much with 
their needles ; but her chief delight was in her book, being addi¢ted to 
read, and well versed in history.” (Sir Hugh Cholmley, Memoirs, 
pp- 49-51 ; 1870.)

368 ‘The entries for 26th and 27th August are remarkable for their 
brevity, considering that they record the visit of the son of Lord Eure 
and a merry hunting party to Hackness, and the ensuing disturbance 
which resulted in a lawsuit before the Council of the North and finally 
before the Court of Star Chamber. (See Introdu¢tion, pp. 40-3.) ‘This 
statement by Sir Tchomas of his case to his cousin, Sir Robert Cecil, in 
a letter of 5th September, 1600, sheds light upon his sensitiveness to the 
attitude towards him of his neighbours. ‘The quarrel was about to be 
heard before the Council of the North, but Hoby was not content that 
the case should come before a Court over which young William Eure’s 
father would be the presiding judge. So he wrote as follows: ‘“‘ Impute 
my presumption to my urgent cause, which for justice’ sake I cannot 
swallow. ‘There has been some dryness in the Lord Ewre (whose 
tenants are my next neighbours) almost ever since I was employed as 
a commissioner in these parts; which, if it has been for my partiality, 
_or injustice, I desire on proof thereof to be punished ; if it be for want of 
partiality (as I shall rather prove) I hope my wrongs will appear in time 
which I have sustained. On 26th August last, his son and brother came 
to my house at Hackness, whose visit I have related in the enclosed 
complaint to the Council, which I beseech you to read and to have 
delivered to the Council. I assure you it is not otherwise for me to 
remain in these parts, nor for any other but their own followers, that 
will fashion justice to their greatness. Ifthe matter may come to judicial 
hearing, I shall prove all my complaint, and shall lay open the partial 
customs of these frozen parts. I crave your pardon for appealing from 
the Council here, which I did in respect of my Lord President’s absence, 
to whom I have sent a copy of the misdemeanour ; and in respect that our 
Vice-President (the Lord Ewre) is father, brother, and cousin to the 
offenders, and who has showed natural affection already in the cause.” 
His letter to the Privy Council related the cause of the quarrel, and 
asked that “ the parties be bound before the Council at York to appear 


270 DIARY 'ORSLA DY HOBBY 


before their Lordships to answer his complaint, for it is not for him to 
serve any process upon them in these parts, in respect of my L. Ewre’s 
greatness, who is the Vice-President, and has summoned him to appear 
at York, to exhibit his complaint, though he is father, brother, and cousin 
to the offenders”. Hoby declared to the Council that the whole business 
proceeded “ from envy and malice for want of partiality in me in the 
executing of my place and calling”. With the letter to the Council 
went the accompanying enclosure: 

“The manner of the riotous assault on Sir Thomas Posthumous 
Hoby, Knight, at his house at Hackness in the North Riding of Yorks, 
by William Ewre, Sir William Ewre, Richard Cholmley, William 
Dawnay, William Hylliarde the younger, Stephen Hutchenson and 
— Smyth, yeoman falkner to the Lord Ewre. 

“On ‘Tuesday the 26th August, Sir Thomas Hoby was standing 
in his hall at Hackness, when there came in Sir William Ewre’s footboy 
and said that his master and sundry other gentlemen would come that 
night. Sir’Thomas answered that he was sorry, his wife was ill and he not 
so well provided for them as he wished, and, desiring the footboy to tell 
his master as much, he answered that his master was hunting in the forest 
of Pyckering Lyth, so as he knew not where to find him. About two 
hours after, the above-named, Mr. Dawnay excepted, came to Hackness 
with sundry other servants and boys, and Sir Thomas, hearing they were 
come into his dining-room, went to them and told them they were 
welcome. 

“Presently, after this, Sir William Ewre’s footboy took forth cards 
and laid them on the table, wherewith some of the gentlemen were 
exercised until supper. In the beginning of supper, Mr Ewre pretending 
he had come to hunt, Sir Thomas sent for his servant that had charge of 
his deer, who dwelt three miles from him, to come the next morning, 
and so continued with them all the time at supper, which was spent by 
the gentlemen partly in discoursing of horses and dogs, sports whereunto 
Sir Thomas never applied himself, partly with lascivious talk where 
every sentence was begun and ended with a great oath, and partly in 
inordinate drinking unto healths, abuses never practised by Sir 
Thomas. 

“In supper time came in a footboy whom they had sent for 
Mr Dawnay, and brought word he would come in the morning. After 
supper, Sir Thomas willed to have their chambers made ready, and came 
himself to bring them to their lodgings, but they being at dice told him 
they would play awhile, so he did leave them, and went down and set his 
household to prayers, as they were accustomed. 

“When Sir Thomas and his family had begun to sing a psalm, the 
company above made an extraordinary noise with their feet, and some 
of them stood upon the stairs at a window opening into the hall, and 


NOTES 271 


laughed all the time of prayers. ‘The next morning they went to breakfast 
in the dining-room, and Sir Thomas, hearing them call for more wine, 
sent for the key of the cellar and told them they should come by no more 
wine from him. Presently Sir Thomas sent to Mr Ewre to know how 
he would bestow that day, and told him if he would leave disquieting 
him with carding, dicing, and excessive drinking, and fall to other sports, 
they should be very welcome. After this message Mr Ewre sent to 
Sir Thomas’s wife that he would see her and be gone, whereunto she 
answered she was in bed and when she was ready she would send him 
word. At his coming, she prayed him to depart the house in quietness, 
and going to the rest of the company, he called a servant of Sir Thomas, 
and said, ‘Tell thy master he hath sent me scurvy messages, and the next 
time I meet him I will tell him so, if he be upon the bench, and will pull 
him by the beard.’ Coming to the uttermost court, Mr Ewre said he 
would go to the top of the hill and fling down mill-stones and would 
play young Devereux, at the same time throwing stones at the windows 
and breaking four quarrels of glass.” Sir ‘Thomas also told the Council 
that this riotous company called him “scurvy urchen ” and “ spindle- 
shanked ape ”’, and other such names in the presence of his wife, and that 
they threatened to fire the town and pull down the parish church. 

On 26th September, in spite of Sir Thomas’s reluctance to submit 
to the Court, the matter was heard before the Council at York, and 
Lord Eure then begged Sir Thomas to accept the protestations of innocence 
of the people concerned, who declared there was no malice in their 
behaviour nor had they meant any insult to Lady Hoby. Lord Eure 
assured Hoby that, at “ the last unkindness”” between him and Eure’s 
son, a year ago, “about his son’s bringing cards into his house,’ when 
Cecil, the all-powerful Secretary, heard of it, he had made the matter 
“a jest to sport at’, and had caused young Eure to imitate his, Hoby’s, 
preacher, “‘ by using such gestures as his preacher did use in his evening 
exercises,” and at which Cecil had laughed heartily. And so, Lord 
Eure ventured to think, he would do this time. But though Hoby 
went away from the Council apparently satisfied, his nature was such 
that the matter was not allowed to rest there, and by November it had 
become a case for the Star Chamber. ‘The proceedings at York are 
related in the evidence given before this court, a record of which still 
exists. 

In January, 1601, Lord Eure sent to Cecil his son’s account of what 
happened at Hackness in August, 1600. Naturally it differs from Hoby’s, 
for it places the blame for anything untoward that occurred upon the 
inhospitable reception of the company by their host, and his ensuing 
churlish treatment of them. It even represents Lady Hoby as trying 
to excuse her husband’s strangeness. Not until 1602 was the case 
settled, presumably in Hoby’s favour, judging from his letters to Cecil.

No record, however, of the verdict has apparently been preserved.

389 The house of her brother-in-law, Sir Edward Hoby (1560-1617), 
Thomas’s elder brother. He was at this time Governor of Queen-

Edward Hoby was educated at Oxford and then entered the Middle 
Temple. Soon, under the auspices of his uncle, Burghley, he advanced 
to high favour at Court, whence he was employed on confidential missions. 
In 1582 he married Margaret, daughter of Henry Carey, Lord Hunsdon, 
and was knighted soon after. He sat in several of Elizabeth’s parliaments, 
and proved himself a good speaker, for he was learned, with an affable 
manner. He was madea J.P. for Middlesex in 1591, and then Constable 
of Queenborough Castle in Sheppey in 1597. In 1601 he became 
a J.P. for Worcestershire. ‘The Queen showed him marked favour in 
various ways, as did also James after his accession. Under him Hoby 
became Groom of the Privy Chamber. An excellent scholar and 
theologian, he cultivated the company of learned men, especially of 
William Camden, who in 1587 dedicated to him his Hidernia. (D.N.B.; 
also Worcestershire County Records, i, p. xix.) 

3 This popular Puritan divine was born in London c. 1555 and 
educated at Cambridge, where he soon gained a reputation for learning. 
He took orders and straightway attached himself to the Puritan party, 
helping to form the presbytery at Wandsworth, Surrey, which has been 
described as the first. presbyterian church in England. In 1584 he was 
suspended for refusing to subscribe to Whitgift’s articles. “The Puritan 
Book of Discipline owed much to him. In 1590 he was summoned 
before the Court of High Commission, and sent to the Fleet for about 
three years. Then, in 1598, he became minister of St. Anne’s, Black- 
friars, where he remained till his death, about 1621. John Manningham 
described him at this time as having ‘‘a little church or chappell vp 
Stayres but a great congregacion, specially of women ”, while Dr. Nowell, 
Dean of St. Paul’s, spoke of Egerton himself as “‘ a man of great learning 
and godliness’’. The preface of a translation of his from the French, 
A Learned and Excellent Treatise . . . contains well-chosen and sensible 
remarks on the choice of reading. Several of his sermons were published 
but few are extant. (D.N.B., art. Stephen Egerton; Diary of Fohn 
Manningham (Cam. Soc., 1868), p. 101.)

415 "This was the Royal Exchange, the shopping centre of London. 
It was founded by Sir Thomas Gresham, and its first stone laid 7th June, 
1566, the building being opened by the Queen on 23rd January, 1572. 
The materials for it were brought from Flanders, and a Flemish builder, 
Henryke, was employed on the work. ‘The Exchange was built in the 
form of a quadrangle, with a cloister running round the interior, and a 
corridor or pawn above. ‘This was divided into stalls: above, were 
attics or bedrooms. On the South or Cornhill front was a bell-tower ; 
on the North, a high Corinthian column, both surmounted by a grass- 
hopper, the crest of the Greshams. ‘The bell was rung at noon and at 
6 p.m. In niches above the covered walk stood statues of the kings and 
queens of England, from Edward the Confessor to Elizabeth. In 1607 
Dekker thus described the Exchange: “ At every turn a man is put in 
mind of Babel, there is such confusion of languages.” In 1631 a con- 
temporary related that, “after the Royal Exchange, which is now called 
the Eye of London, had been builded two or three years, it stood in a 
manner empty; and a little before her Majesty was to come thither to 
view the beauty thereof, and to give it a name, Sir Thomas Gresham. 


276 DIARY OF LADY HOBY 


in his own person, went, twice in one day, round about the upper pawn, 
and besought those few shopkeepers then present that they would furnish 
and adorn with wares and wax lights as many shops as they could or 
would, and they should have all those shops so furnished rent free that 
year, which otherwise at that time was 40s. a shop by the year; and 
within two years after he raised that rent unto four marks a year; and 
within a while after that he raised his rent of every shop unto £4 10s. 
a year, and then all shops were well furnished according to that time; 
for then the milliners or haberdashers in that place sold mouse-traps, 
bird-cages, shoeing horns, lanthorns, and Jews’ trumps, etc. ‘There 
were also at that time that kept shops in the upper pawn of the Royal 
Exchange, armourers that sold both old and new armour, apothecaries, 
booksellers, goldsmiths, and glass-sellers, although now it is as plenteously 
stored with all kinds of rich wares and fine commodities as any particular 
place in Europe, into which many foreign princes daily send to be best 
served of the best sort”. The Exchange was destroyed in the Great 
Fire of 1666. (Wheatley, H. B. and Cunningham, P., London Past 
and Present, iil, pp. 182-3. 1891.)

422 Strype says that a Venetian, James Verselyn, is said to have been

under the Great Seal. But the Glass-Sellers of London were much 
aggrieved, and represented to the Privy Council that it meant the over- 
throw of fifty households, which used “ only the trade of selling of glasses ”’, 
for there was a clause in the patent that none should sell such glass but 
Verselyn himself. ‘“‘ Like the glass-house furnace in Blackfriars, the 
bone-fires that are kept there never go out, inasmuch that all the inhabitants 
are almost broyled like carbonadoes with the sweating sickness,” wrote

434 John Udall, the puritan, entered Cambridge in 1578, where he 
became the friend and fellow-student of John Penry. Before 1584, 
he was the incumbent of Kingston-on-Thames. In 1586 he was 
prosecuted for his hostility to episcopacy, and in July, 1588, deprived 
of his benefice. He then became a preacher at Newcastle-upon-Tyne, 
but in 1590 was imprisoned on suspicion of complicity in the Marprelate 
tracts and, in 1591, condemned to death. A year later the pardon 
came, but Udall died very soon afterwards. James [ is said to have 
considered him the greatest scholar in Europe, and his Diotrephes or 
The State of the Church, a tra& published anonymously in 1588, showed 
him to have humour as well as scholarship. ‘This work occupies an 
important place in the history of the Marprelate controversy as well 
as a niche in the history of literature. Udall published many sermons in 
his life-time, but his Commentary on the Lamentations of Feremiah, and 
a Hebrew Grammar and Didionary appeared after his death. (D.N.B.; 
Camb. Hist. of Eng. Lit.; Short-Title Catalogue of English Books, etc.)

486 "The Book of Discipline set forth the Puritan views on Church 
government and teaching. Stephen Egerton helped largely in its 
promotion. 

487 "This was a court of special sessions held for the inhabitants of the 
manor of Hackness by the owner of the manor, and attended by J.P.s 
from the immediate distri@. "There were two kinds of manor courts ; 
the Court Leet, which dealt mainly with smaller police matters, and the 
Court Baron, which was the customary court of the tenants of the manor, 
and was concerned mainly with questions regarding the land. Hackness 
was situated in the distriét which comprised the Liberty of Whitby 
Strand. The Liberty had once belonged, land and jurisdiction over it, 
to the Abbey of Whitby. ‘The land, with the exception of Hackness, 
went by purchase, some years after the surrender of the Abbey to the 
Crown, to the Cholmley family of Whitby, and they had held courts 
for the Liberty from that time, alleging the jurisdiction to have been 
included in the deal. ‘T’o these courts had come the inhabitants of the 
Liberty, and of Hackness too. They had attended, the steward of the 


NOTES 281 


Cholinleys asserted, from Whitby, Newholm, Dunsley, East Rowe, 
Ruswarp, Stakesby, Ewe Cote, Eskdaleside, Sleights, Ugglebarnby, 
Sneaton, Sneaton Thorpe, Hawsker, Stainsacre, Larpool, Whitby Lathes, 
Robin Hood’s Bay, Fylingdales, Hackness, Suffield, Everly, Harwood 
Dale, Silpho and Broxa. But the right of the Cholmleys to hold these 
courts for the Liberty was not clear, and when Sir Thomas Hoby took 
up his residence at Hackness on his wife’s property, and their title to 
the manor was confirmed to them, Hoby held an independent court 
for his own manor, and even made it his business to contest the right 
of the Cholmleys to hold any other court than that for their own manor 
of Whitby. He even went so far as to sue to the Crown for the grant to 
himself for life of the jurisdiction of the Liberty of Whitby Strand. This, 
naturally, gave rise to a long law-suit, finally decided by arbitrators, 
who, by their decision, allowed the owner of Hackness to hold his own 
courts leet and baron, but upheld the claim of the Cholmleys to hold the 
courts for the Liberty of Whitby Strand. (V.C.H., Yorks., N.R., ii, p. 503. 
Bihar Dozs.ii Frid: vole xliv, | No. 166)5,,xlv,No..87.3)) lxvin,No.(101s) 
Special or Petty Sessions were also held twice a year in each wapentake 
of the North Riding. ‘These were attended by the J.P.s who lived in 
the district, ranging in number from two to five, and by the sheriff’s 
deputy. Amongst the North Riding Quarter Sessions Records, the 
earliest date of the records of Special Sessions at Hackness which have 
been preserved is 1620. Sir Thomas Posthumous Hoby and Sir Richard 
Etherington were the presiding Justices. (North Riding Quarter Sessions 
Records, li, pp. 230-1.)

Bernard described York as the “ principale Cité d’Angleterre apres 
celle de Londres. Le conseil reside 14 pour pourvoir aux affaires 
Septentrionales du costé d’Escosse . . . York est sise sur la riuiere de 
Humber. Ceste riuiere est fort aggrandie par les riuieres de Dune, et de 
Trente, tombans en icelle. Au deca de Humber vers le midy le pais 
est fertille et plaisant. Au dela en tirant vers Escosse, il est métagneux et 
plain de vallees. Es vallees et pres des riuieres les Gentilshommes ont 
leurs mais6s basties et accomodees, pour euiter l’orage et les grads vents, 
a quoy l’Angleterre est naturellement subiecte.” ‘This last sentence 
is a fitting description of the situation of Hackness in its sheltered hollow.

as Bernard from York to London, but his mileage is also different. He

and great trades of merchandize therin vsed. ‘There be in the citie of

561 On 29th May, all not in special attendance, or with need to 
remain, at Court, were ordered to leave London, on account of the plague, 
not later than the end of the term, 6th June, and not to return until 
the Coronation. ‘This was because James was alarmed at the multitudes 
besieging the court, and the mounting totals of the weekly bills of mortality 
for London and the liberties. “The plague had begun in London in 
March. It was thought to have been brought over from the Low 
Countries, though London was seldom free from it owing to the lack 
of sanitation and extensive dealings with the Levant and European 
countries. “The sickness, however, attracted little attention before 
April, and then the infection began alarmingly to increase, and by June 
the grip of the plague on the city was firm. (Wilson, F. P., The Plague 
in Shakespeare's London, pp. 86, et seq.)

564 ‘The details of this conspiracy have not yet been made clear, nor 
the guilt of the Ruthvens adequately proved. ‘The story, as told by 
James and his adherents, is, briefly, that the King, while staying at 
Falkland for the hunting, was enticed, on a trumped-up pretext, to the 
town-house in Perth of John Ruthven, the third Earl of Gowrie, and 
his brother Alexander, on 5th August, 1600. From this trap to kidnap 
him, James escaped only after a scuffle in which the two brothers were 
killed. This story has been doubted, because it suited the King well 
to be rid of these two brothers with their claim to the throne, and a bad

family record of conspiracy and plot. James ordered the date to be kept 
ever after as a public holiday in memory of his escape. A special form of 
prayer was issued for use upon this day. (See Records of the Old Arch- 
deaconry of St. Albans (St. Albans and Herts. Architectural and Archaeol. 
Soc.), p. 132. 1908.) ‘There is in the original a space between this entry

585 "T"his was the elder of the two sons of Sir Michael Stanhope, 
who were both called Edward. This man had represented first 
Nottinghamshire and then Yorkshire in Parliament, was a surveyor of 
the Duchy of Lancaster, the Recorder of Doncaster, and a member of 
the Council of the North. He was buried at Kirby Warfe in Yorkshire. 
There are frequent allusions to him in the diary, and he had much to 
do with Margaret’s courtship by Hoby. 

566 ‘The plague deaths showed an increase of over a thousand a week 
during the fortnight ending 4th August, in which James had had his 
coronation. After that the returns dropped one hundred and seventy- 
nine. (Wilson, F. P., T4e Plague in Shakespeare’s London, pp.92-3. 1927.) 

567 A small space is left after this entry. 

568 Wednesday, roth August, was ordered to be kept as a holiday 
and a fast throughout the kingdom; and on every Wednesday following, 
while the plague lasted, special prayers were offered, sermons of repentance 
preached, and collections made for the poor. On fast days, “ all Persons 
(children, olde, weake and sicke folks, and necessaire Harvest labourers, 
or the like excepted) are required to eate . . . but one competant and 
moderate Meale, and that towards night after Evening prayer : observing 
sobrietie of diet without superfluitie of ryotous fare, respecting necessitie 
and not voluptuousnesse.” (Quoted Wilson, F. P., op. cit., p. 99.) 

The influx of visitors to London in conneétion with the accession of 
the new sovereign, and their subsequent dispersal, no doubt helped to 
spread and increase the plague. By August conditions in London were 
dreadful; one out of every six was sick or dying, nor could regulations 
be enforced under such conditions. Moreover, as a contemporary 
wrote, “ the absence of the Aldermen from the City, and the Justices in 
the shire (inferior officers being not regarded) hath bred liberty, and 
scope in their lamentable cases and disorders.”” ‘The rich had fled, 
together with physicians and surgeons, and all restraints were broken. 
Infected men, women, and children thrust themselves recklessly into the 
company of others, and seemed to strive to convey their sickness by 
allowing the accumulation in the streets of all sorts of personal rubbish. 
Pitiable scenes were common. 

** Whole housholds, and whole streets are stricken, 
‘The sick do die, the sound do sicken, 
And Lord have mercy upon us, crying 
Ere Mercy can come forth, th’ are dying. 


NOTES 287 


No musick now is heard but bells, 

And all their tunes are sick men’s knells ; 

And every stroake the bell does toll, 

Up to heaven it windes a soule.” 
With the approach of colder days, the plague diminished, but did not 
altogether disappear during the whole of the following winter. London 
recovered, however, with astounding rapidity from the confusion and 
misery which had for a time completely overwhelmed it. By the end 
of January the number of deaths had fallen to fifteen, but they did not 
quite cease, continuing even into June. In 1604 London was the 
healthiest place in the country, for elsewhere, as is plain from this diary, 
the plague raged fiercely. (Ibid., pp. roo-118.)

578 Lady Hoby may refer here to what is now the earliest known Bill 
of Mortality extant for London. It was for the week ending 2oth 
Odtober, 1603. It is reproduced, from a copy in the Guildhall, in 
Wilson’s The Plague in Shakespeare's London (p. 106), and also in 
Christie’s Some Account of Parish Clerks. In addition to giving the 
figures for the week for the parishes within and without the walls, and 
for the out-parishes, the bill adds “‘ the true Relation of the whole number 
of all that have died of this Visitation, from the 17. of December, 
1602, to the 20 of this present moneth of October 1603, with the 
whole number of all that have dyed in Westminster, in the Savoy, in 
Stepney, Newington, and sundry other places, since the sicknesse began 
there.” 

The returns given in these bills of mortality are substantially accurate, 
and are in general agreement with parish registers, but returns for the 
plague alone are not so trustworthy. From 23rd December, 1602-22nd 
December, 1603, 38, 244 people died in London, 30,758 being of the 
plague. If the deaths in the places mentioned above are added, the 
total is 43,154, of which 35,104 were of the plague. ‘The population 
of London at the time can hardly have been greater than 250,000. 
(Wilson, op. cit., pp. 189, et seq.)

_ THE DIARY OF 


LADY MARGARET 
HOBY 


GO 


This diary of an English gentlewoman of over three 
hundred years ago reveals, as nothing else can, the 
actual pursuits and pastimes, the religious feeling 
and intellectual powers of women of Tudor Eng- 
land. Lady Hoby, who lived in the delightful old 
village of Hackness on the river Derwent, was a wo- 
man of breeding, active ‘mind, and substantial cul- 
ture. Hers is the earliest Englishwoman’s diary in 
existence, and it has never before been published. 


The First Woman Diarist 


HOUGHTON MIFFLIN CO.
